IVIcDOUGALL'S Pateqt SELF-RAISING FLOUR The Examiner,

"11is gone the round, so I ileet mind idling yoit,'• retorted the heiress, magnanimously. "It signified ' A Tale of Two Cities " " I confess that I do not follow you," the young man said. "Well, 'dam ' is the tail of Amsterdam and Rotterdam, don't you see ?" " Oh, yes, I see now," said Harold, slowly; " but, excuse me, isn't it rather far fetched? It I remember aright, the cities in which the action of the novel takes place are London and Paris." The heiress pouted. "It seems that my little efforts are not to your fastidious taste, Mr. Car- stairs; so let us talk of someone else," she said. "Tell me what does that match mean, which Mr. Ramsden is wearing for a scarf pin ?" The only other male member of the company was sitting very near, and Harold saw that the match was a partially ignited one. Seeing that ideas ran in one grove, he had no difficulty in discovering it to signify An Unequal match, and told his questioner so. " Really, Mr. Carstairs, I shall have to ask you to make me out a nice long list, you are quite an acquisition. Won't you tell me your own title? I'll vote for it all the same."

" Yes," answered the girl, demurely : and they went. The mellow radiance of autumn was every- where around them, and the majestic river rolled at their feet, as it had in the days of their child- hood. Nothing had changed since they parted at this season, they thought, as they sat on a grassy knoll under the mighty trees high on the bank, watching the teams draw the wagons along the white road across the water. Harold told his news, but, as he warmed to the thought of what it might possibly mean to him, th., advice he asked was quite of a different nature to that which he had intended. "Do you think, Hilda, that—if I —got this money, and went abroad to get new impressions for another volume—l might, when I came back, find my little friend something, you know, just a little more. . . 0, Hilda, I love you, and can't go away without sayinv, it. I'm not a mercenary beggar. I'm willing to work like a horse, if you'll only give me a hope." "Then you intend to go after all," faltered the girl, "just because I've got more money at present than you have." " Well, Hilda, it's a bit awkward, isn't it : what would people think of me ?" "It's not nearly so awkward as your speeches," said Hilda, trying to laugh, but breaking into a sob. In a moment Harold's arms were around her, and money, the world, the future were for- gotten, as the beating of their happy hearts drowned the river's rolling tide in their ears. Perhaps Harold will learn some day whose loving belief in him, and farsighted policy prompted the measure which gave a fillip to the sale of his book, and brought his merits before a discerning public. At present the arch plotter was resting her head contentedly against hie shoulder, comforting herself with the reflection that, if wives are morally bound to tell their husbands everything, sweethearts are no so fet- tered with regard to their lovers. " Dearest," said Harold, pressing her to him, as they walked to the boat—of course he had to sustain her by the waist, the banks being steep and treacherous with yellow mud—" I looked upon the ordeal of Mrs. Pilkington's Book Tea with despair—" "Well, you didn't even get the booby' prize," interrupted the girl archly. "No, but it lead to my winning the greatest prize in the world—yourself, sweetheart." Allowing for a lover's ecstasy, the speech was sot unduly extravagant.

"I am bound by secrecy," said Harold, who was anxious to get away, and thought she would dismiss him. The hope was vain, she had still an interest to serve. "Do you know my title, Mr. Carstairs ?" she said, with an air of superiority ; "it's beneath your nose." Harold scanned her up and down, and saw she was wearing two tiny padlocks to fasten her shoes. The allusion was evident. " _Locke on the Understandiny, I presume," he remarked. The heiress was decidedly displeased. "Really, you are too dreadfully clever," she said. "How- ever, the gentlemen don't vote, so it doesn't matter."

Harold was disgusted with her self interest and supercilious manner, and rose to move away, saying that he must see if his sister wanted a cup of tea. Mrs. Odell said eagerly, "Well F' " and he rejoined " impossible," with an accent there was no mistaking. His sister walked oft after a brief interval of speaking silence, leaving him to his own reflections, so he amused himself with scanning the devices of the guests, as they moved hither and thither before him. His eyes at length met another pair fixed on him, eyes (hat were vaguely familiar, although he could not recall the face. Their owner was a beautiful dark girl, of slender, lithe figure, and about twenty-four years of are. She smiled at him, opsitively smiled, frankly, and made a slight beckoning movement so that he was forced to go over to her, saying to himself meanwhile—" I must have a double somewhere : hope I shan't be snubbed for au unfortunate likeness." " Is it possible that you have quite forgotten me, Mr. Carstairs ?" said the girl, as he stood before her.

Of the 7,116 persons who during the winter have regis- tered themselves at the Manchester Corporation Labour Bureau 2,959 were found employment.

"I ought to know the eyes," responded Harold, "but am ashamed to confess that ,I cannot " . . .

"Cannot recognise the girl you vowed to love, and cherish, and protect, when she grew up. Not know the maiden you were to make your wife—squaw, I think you said; our home was to be on the bounding prairie, I remember. Is there nothing left of the scraggy child you fished out of the pond, put your jacket around, and carried on your back to the house P' "Hilda Raybould, by all that's wonderful. What an ass I am! But you know" . . He halted lamely, but the admiring, compre- hensive look must have been quite readable and satisfactory to the girl. " My name is Hurst, now," said she. Harold looked at her, blankly. " Married," said be, with a falling inflection. " Neither married nor likely to be, though I am a rich woman. An uncle in London died, and I had to take his surname with the money." " 0," said Harold, brightening, relieved, he scarcely knew why. " Now tell me all about yourself," said she ; and, nothing loth, he did, seeming to drop easily into the old childish confidence with his former playfellow. But their pleasant confabulation was broken by the voting. Hilda was out of it all, she was recently down from London, knew nothing of the nature of the function, and, as a stranger, wore no badge. Mrs. Odell carried off first honours with Round the Red Lamp—which everyone had thought to be 31-oths; but, although Harold's blank card, which meant No lame, had not been generally understood, the 'booby prize' went to Mr. Ramsden, owing to the champion- ship of the mortified Miss Singleton whom many people wished to please. Mrs. Odell came up jubilant, and Harold in- troduced the two ladies, which ended in an in- vitation to Hill View, for Hilda, and an elderly cousin who was playing the part of chaperon. Harold was enthusiastic over the girl, as he es- corted his sister home, and this lady, mindful of her last failure, heard him patiently, but gave no advice.

The acquaiutance thus made and renewed, ripened into friendship all round, for the Odells sincerely admired the frank, high-spirited girl, whilst the cousin, Miss Martin, was no soared old maid, but a pleasant, cultured woman who would have been a welcome addition to any circle. As for the young people themselves, they fell naturally into the old life of comradeship, save that there was now no talk of sweethearting, for Harold was no fortune-hunter, and accepted the altered conditions without grumbling at his lot. There were pie-nics, tennis parties, drives, and he found these amusements delightful in her company, but their great pleasure was to take train to Oakle Street, walk to Westbury-on- Severn, and there hire a boat to ride the broad waters of the river past the hamlets in which their early holidays had been spent ; or to cross to Longney, by the ferry, and wander amongst the orchard lands there, now glorious with ripen- ing fruit. But who could experience these reminiscences of the happy past without feeling the full spirit which the scenes suggested ? Certainly not two high-spirited young people with an undeter- minate future, and without binding home ties, particulary when the man was a traveller with the mystery of foreign lands and adventure clothing him as a garment, and the girl repre- sented all that is charming in the woman of leisure.

When the truth forced itself upon Harold that he was looking upon Hilda with something more than the eyes of friendship, he comforted himself with the idea that his book might bring him fortune, and so put him out of the category of lovers whose views are believed to be entirely mercenary. But it did nothing of the kind. Messrs. Bur- man, the publishers, brought out the work on the royalty system, it is true, but it hung fire, somehow, and Harold saw nothing before him but another exile to a laud where he could put his shoulder to the wheel at employments im- possible in his native land. The idea of departure, not the idea of labour, made him dispirited and gloomy, and the girl was not slow to observe the change. "What- ever are you looking so rueful about, Harold ?" she asked, suddenly one day. " Got to go into the world again," said he, "to try gold digging, stock droving, or some- thing equally romantic—in tales—but precious prosaic when you leave behind you all the places —and persons you love best." " 0," said the girl, the colour leaving her face, and there was a painful silence for a space. Then she said, "What about the book? I think it charming. Those descriptions of the ancient ruins, unmentioned by previous travellers, are enthralling, to say nothing of the pains you have taken to obtain the rites and customs of the Matabele, the specimens of their language you aim and the fauna and flora of the country." "Thank you, Jdllda, you're a brick. But the public don't take to the book. I'm afraid. There have been one or two good reviews, but the sales are very slow. I must confess to a failure, and go and work with my hands." " And my stay is over in a fortnight. You won't go before then, Harold, I hope ? I want to see the fruit gathered at Longney, and witness the merry-making." " We'll go,, Said Harold, brightening. " I wouldn't miss our last glimpse of the old days together for the brightest future in the world Joseph Shaw, a ten-year-old Eccles boy' stole a birched. trowel, polishe it up, pawned it, and bought two toy alone." Wouldn't you ?" asked the girl, colourin pistols. He had been reading penny dreadfuls, and was " g, but she looked at him direct with the eyes he loved so well. It was a great effort to restrain his feelings at this juncture, but he put the pos- session by, as unmanly. "I wouldn't," he re- peated simply, but there was a catch in his • voice, and he turned his head away. When he Fels-Naptha soap—half the labour looked round again, Hilda was gone. of washing, and washes better in About five days after, he received an unex- pected communication from his publishers. "Let us go down the river to-day, to our fav- ourite ' said Harold to Hilda: " I've some Rood news, and want to ask vonr advice."

MRS. PILKINGTON'S BOOK TEA,

DRUID GRAYL.

"And what on earth is a Book Tea ?" said Harold Carstairs to his sister Mrs. Odell. " I've heard of a meat tea, and other abominations beloved of the feminine system. Does one write one's name in a confession-album, and give par- ticulars as to a fancy respecting Dohea or Son- chong, concomitant with buttered scone or almond cake ?"

"Don't be so laboriously witty and superior, Hal," said his sister : " you have lived so long among roaring cannibals as to have quite lost touch with decent civilized society." " The Matabele are not cannibals, and have the softest language in the world ; much more liquid than the Italian, even," responded her brother.

The young man was just home from Matabele- land, with a manuscript volume of travels which he was seeking a publisher for—as yet vainly. The family estate at Ashleworth had dwindled away owing to the agricultural depression and unfortunate speculation during the last years of the father's lifetime, and the son had gone into the world to try to make his way, and retrieve the position : his sister's future being secured by her marriage with Mr. Odell, a high official in the Custom House, and it was at their residence, Hill View, The Spa, Gloucester, that he was temporarily staying. Said his sister, " Never mind the Matabele and their language. I shall be able to read it all when your book comes out. I intend you to escort me to Mrs. Pilkington's, to-morrow after- noon, so please allow rne to post you up in the latest diversion, and don't interrupt with your rude observations again." " All right, Sis, fire away, I apologize," said Harold, meekly. "Each person attending a Book Tea wears a badge which displays a sort of rebus on the name of a popular novel, or well-known work of litera- ture—so your hook is tabooed, you perceive." " Who's making rude observations, now ?" zaked her brother, mildly. " I am, just to mlow you how unpleasant it is. Tit for tat, and start fresh," responded Mrs. Odell. " Now you ought to be able to give me some good ideas, you've read so many books ; and let me tell you that there are pretty nick- nacks as prizes for the people whose titles are not found out, or those whose clever ideas in the way of design are voted by the majority of guests present to be the most worthy. There's also a booby prize ' for the gentleman whose badge is the most ridiculous—a prize such as an em- broidered tobacco-pouch, pipe and case and 'other abominations beloved by the masculine system.' " "Go on, you resentful—woman," said Harold. " I thought you agreed to make a fresh start, unmindful of my original indiscretion." "I really couldn't resist that last speech: you men are so selfish with your pipes and baccas ' —flake, navy-cut and B.D.V : I've beard the names you see. John's vocabulary, in that direc- tion, is just as copious as yours." As Mr. Odell usually smoked cigars of fine brand, and had only run over the titles so that his wife might " keep her end up " with some samples of callow youth she was brought in con- tact with—to earn the reputation of " deuced clever woman, Mrs. Odell, don't you know, un- derstands a fellow's ways, and all that "—he was not being treated with justice. But he wouldn't have minded ; he was the most easy-going, kind- hearted of men, and, when released from official decorum, could unbend and become a jovial com- panion. In fact he had won more than one " booby prize " solely on his social memo. " Get on to the Book Tea ; initiate re into the mysteries or I'll go and smoke to torment yon," said Harold Caristairs.

" Well, a word or two to that effect won't be amiss," replied his sister. "I want you to be distinctly original, for there's a rich heiress, Rosa Singleton, who is sure to be present, and makes the thing a craze : and one never knows what might happen." Harold groaned. "I see what you are leading up to," he sail, " Can't you let me enjoy my liberty l You're a born match-maker I know, Sis, but you know the adage about driving a horse to the water."

" I know that for years there hasn't been a horse at Ashleivorth, to drink," said his sister quietly. "You don't always intend to be lord of a barren, curtailed heritage, I hope." " By Jove, no," sighed Harold. "But there's my book, you know." "Bother your book, let's get on to the other books," said his sister, who having planted her shaft home, was content. "Now I've told you that Miss Singleton makes quite a craze of the amusement. I mention her name again only to show its possibilities ; for she's really clever. For instance, last time we met, she wore a nar- row letter A, in scarlet, which ran from top to bottom of her dress. Guess what it signified." " The Scarlet Letter, I should say," responded her brother, promptly. "Just what everyone else thought, but quite incorrectly. It meant Innocents Abroad: do you see it ? In no sense A broad. Wasn't it clever ?" "There was an ingenuity about it worthy of a better cause : she ought to be writing burlesques where puns are welcome. I thought they were ousted from modern society." "Not in a case such as the present, stupid," responded Mrs. Odell with true sisterly frank- ness. "On another occasion she wore a card with a grub inside a corn-cob painted on it. Can you guess that F" " I can't," remarked her brother. " It's bs• pond me. I quite perceive that I am the un- tutored savage you made me out to be." "It meant Within the Maze. See maize ' and ' maze 'F Clever again. Admit it." "Another pun." " It's inevitable. What is a rebus but a pic- torial pun ?" "I always thought there was a difference be- tween a riddle and a mere conundrum."

"Well, as you're so clever, perhaps you'll in- vent yourself and me something distinctly novel. I haven't won a prize yet: my poor little puzzles have teen solved straightway. I've been so des- pairing that I almost went on one occasion with my blouse inside out to represent The Seamy Side. You may dress for the title, or wear a mere label, as you please, you know." " I'm very glad you didn't," said her brother indignantly. He shared the old-fashioned pre- judice against a woman making an undignified exhibition of herself. But, when he came to consider the laws of the game, he found himself more hampered than he expected. As his sister had said, the pun asserted itself persistently. It was hours before he could solve the difficulty, apart from this form of IA it, and even when he had hit upon a change, the re- sult did not strike him as being at all satisfactory. However, he was led to a neighbouring house, like a lamb to the slaughter, carrying in his poc- ket a blank label for himself, whilst his sister wore a picture of a semaphore with the danger signal showing, surrounded by moths. He was introduced to his hostess, and to several other ladies present, including the heiress, who was certainly enthusiastic, not to say tiresome— she was so saturated with the business in band that she appeared to know nothing of the works whose titles she was so glib with. "Look around, do, Hr. Carstairs," she said , "are you puzzled ?I am not. See that lady with the word ebb in a knot? That's The Ebb Tide, I'm positive." "Fine novel," said Carstairs , "contains some of Stevenson's best work, I think. Do you? That scene—" "I haven't read it," she interrupted. "It takes me all my time to study a library cata- logue, to keep pace with competitors. Isn't it an interesting pastime? Much better than gossip and music. We are to have Music Teas soon, though, and then Play Teas; then of course I shall have to look up music sheets and play bills. That girl over there with the letter 0 sewn on her shoulder, has not puzzled me. The title is I've an o,7raiiitoe, you know." _ _

CO-EDUCATION.

HOW THE SYSTEM IS WORKING AT PENKETH FRIENDS' SCHOOL. --- Vice-Chancellor Dale Opens the New wing,

Founded in the year of the accession of Queen Victoria, the Co-educational Boarding School at Penketh, which belongs to the Society of Friends, has gained an honoured place among local educational institutions, and there was a large gathering on Saturday to witness the formal opening of the new wing which has recently been added to the premises. The system on which the school is conducted is of special interest and almost unique. The boys and girls are taught in mixed classes and, in the words of the headmaster (Mr. William Edward Brown, B.A.),share the same privileges and penalties. They are equally eligible for membership in the literary society, reading circle, field club, or any otter assomaton belonging to the corporate life of the school. So far as possible, they join in the same games. The special objects which the founders of the school had in view are somewhat quaintly set forth in the original document, which states that it was the intention "to establish a school for the purpose of giving a religious, moral, and useful education to children brought up in the pxinciples and practices of our religious Society." It was also the design of the founders to" combine labour with instruction, and thereby to promote habits of industry in the children, as well as for the'emoloyment of the boys in manual labour or handicraft trades, and the girls in housewifery." It is a source of pride and encouragement to those who interest themselves or receive instruction in the school that John Bright, although not a scholar in the building, was taught in a house quite near. The institution has carried on an , eminently useful work and, to keen pace with the times, every effort is being made to adapt it to modern conditions.

The new wing, which was formally opened on Saturday by Vice-Chancellor Dale, of Liverpool University, includes a science laboratory fitted up for practial work in chemistry and elementary physics, two dormitories for boys' a set of music rooms, and better accommodation for the teaching staff. The old block of buildings comprises a gymnasium, work- shops, open-air swimming bath, lecture hall, museum and library, beside the usual Boarding School require- ments, and the playing fields cover some eight acres 'of ground. The cost of the extension is £2,600.

===== 0002601_19050401_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
lost. He did not think, however, that they need fear that the high ideal of education which the Friends had followed—and which was concentrated in the word character—would ever be lost sight of in the future.

The Headmaster then presentgd an interesting report on the work of the school. He pointed out that for many years places had been found for children quite unconnected with the Society of Friends. The number of pupils in the school at present was 61-34 boys and 27 girls. About 40 of the scholars were members of the Society of Friends or were connected with it ; the rest were unconnected. In the earlier years there were two separate schools under the same roof, but the barriers between them had been gradually removed until few traces remained. The tradition of the past, when boys and girls were kept rigidly apart, must for a time hinder the full realization of the co-educational ideal, but he hoped that as time went on the social life of the institution would become more and more unified. The curriculum included the usual subjects of a secondary school course. Mr. Brown de- tailed the gratifying list of successes gained by the students and pointed out that the difficulty in raising the standard of instruction lay partly in the fact that many pupils entered the school at the age of thirteen with only elementary school knowledge and did not remain there sufficiently long. Vi,e-Chancellor Dale, at the commencement of his address, said he felt he owed one of the best and most important parts of his education to that great and patriotic Englishman of whom every Friend had a right to be proud—John Bright. During the 1868 election he as a schoolboy had the opportunity of hearing every speech which John Bright delivered in a campaign of six or seven weeks' duration. He ven- tured to say in all seriousness that he believed that in the speeches he heard that summer he laid as solid and as valuable a foundation on which to build the educa- tion of later life as he could possibly have laid in any other way. He could not but remember all the works of beneficence, compassion, practical loving-kindness and human service for which The Society of , Friends had such a noble record. In offering his congratulations upon the extension, the Vice- Chancellor contrasted the healthy situation of the Friends' school with that of some town schools in close and noisy streets. He was glad the children at Penketh were spending their school days in a place where there was pure and wholesome air and where they would lay up a store of that health which was such a priceless possession when they came to face and to bear the strain of work and duty. The headmasterlhad referred to the exceptional character of the school. For his own part he (the Vice-Chancellor) threw overboard the technical word and he would simply describe it as a school in which boys and girls were taught together in the same class-room and played together in the same field. Once given the system,he thoroughly agreed that the further it could be carried—carefully, watchfully. and soberly—the better for all concerned. He would not say, however, that the education of boys and girls together was the only right way. One of the great and most obvious defects of the English system:of education had been the tendency to adhere too servilely and slavishly to a single type of school. We ought to hope

===== 0002601_19050401_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
f great things from larger variety, and experiment of any I kind was not only justiflable,but wise and helpful. Just now, when the whole of the education system was about to be transformed, the experience of such a school as Ponketh was exceptionally interesting. The conditions under which the education system in many places was being reshaped meant that it would be often impossible to get a good secondary school unless it was a mixed school. He spoke of the absolute necessity of lengthen- ing the normal course if the school was to do all the good of which it was capable. Continuing, the Vice- Chancellor emphasised the fact that a school was not a place in which they collected a certain amount of in- formation but a place where they developed and built up certain :habits— of thought, feeling, and action, the sum and substance of which they might fully describe by the single word character. The speaker showed what a powerful influence tra- ditions, conscience and ideals should have on school life. If they could only add one half to the ordinary course of the ordinary child at school, he believed they would double the effect which forces of the kind he had named had upon the children. When- ever ore saw an institution like the Penketh school developing itself and adapting itself to the new condi- tions of new times it filled one with courage and with hope. "The supreme conflicts of the coming years " said the Vice-Chancellor "are not going to be the conflicts of armies and of fleets. The forts in which , our fathers :trusted for defence are for the most part grey ruins or grassy turf. I am convinced that the strongholds of the future will be the schools of the nation. (Applause.) I believe it because the school, as I understand it, is not merely a place of preparation for the business or profession, still less a mere intellectual gymnasium, but its purpose is something higher and loftier. It is a place in which the powers of our children are developed and trained for service; in which a noble character is built up; and in which our little ones are prepared for the citizenship both of an eatddy and a heavenly kingdom. I take it that that is the purpose and the aim of this school; that that was the thought in the minds of the good men who founded it ; that this is the thought and the purpose oi* those wile maintain it and guide it now and of all of us who meet beneath this roof this afternoon. I trust that this work will be an enduring work and that in the harvest of the future it will be abundantly blessed." (Applause.) Mr. J. W. Graham, M.A., Principal of Dalton Hall, Manchester, also addressed the gathering, and said the opening of the new wing was an epoch-making step. No one belonging to the Society of Friends, he con- tended,ought to have less than a secondary education.By no means must the idea be prevalent amongst them that whichwas called an elementary education was good enough for anyone within their borders. They had a duty not only to the Society but to the nation andithey would have to regard their schools in connection with the national system of education more and more. In regard to ' Government grants, they were at present slight and ' unsystematic, and if it suited a school to earn them there was no harm in its doing so. If in time to come we got a uniform national system of education, religious but free from

" OBSERVER " CUP. SEMI-FINAL. WARRINOTON ALBIONS V LATCHFORD BRITANNIA. Great interest was taken in this match, which was played on Walker's Brewery ground last Saturday, and 2,000 spectators assembled. Albions were at full strength, but Brittannia haOseveral of their usual com- bination men absent. The Albion players were full of energy, and after three minutes' play the ball came to the foot of Houghton, who opened the scoring account with a swift, low shot. Stun.. by this reverse Britannia tried hard to get away, but the Albion forwards came again in a dashing manner, and goal No. 2 was registered by Woods. Houghton obtained the leather, and passed out to Broster, who after tricking several] opponents centred. The leather, however, struck several of the Britannia men, but i t finally came to Glover, who scored. Hardly had the game restarted than the winners were again busily attacking, and Glover further increased the score. Half-time : Albions, four goals ; Britannia, nil. Latchford improved wonderfully in the second half , and were more persistent on the attack. There was, however, no further scoring. Final : Albions, four goals ; Britannia, nil.

HOSPITAL SUNDAY.

Rev. C. Harvey Cook and Workingmen's Efforts.

The congregations attending the various places of worship in Warrington on Sunday were once more afforded an opportunity of showing their appreciation of the noble work of the Infirmary and Dispensary by con- tributing to its funds. The need for liberal help was perhaps never more urg,..nt than at the present time, for it is obvious that the continuous increase in the number of patients must soon demand a scheme of enlargement. It is hoped that the response to the appeals from the pulpit will show an improvement upon previous years. The REV. MORLEY STEVENSON, principal of the Training College, preached at the Parish Church on Sunday morning and contrasted the condition of the world, before Christ came and afterwards. Before Jesus Christ came, he said, the sick were absolutely on- cared for unless they had wealth. If a person was ill his friends and neighbours deserted him and left him to die. Any idea of ian institution in which the sick could be cared for was absolutely unknown. Sickness and disease were met with a callous, a heartless and cruel indifference. Christ by example healed the sick, and He cared for the bodies of the people quite as much as he did for their souls. He did not turn away from loathsome diseases. The Christian religion had brought us the Christian physician, who often enough gave his services without pay, visited the homes of the poor and brought with him life and cheerfulness. People like Florence Nightingale in her work in the Crimea, Sister Dora in her work in the " Black country," Father Damier in his work among the lepers, testified to the result of the3eaching of Christ. Now, if they looked round Christian England,they would see no callousness, no cruelty, but on all hands efforts were being made to comfort and heal. All was due to the leaven of the teaching of Jesus Christ. With reference to our own Infirmary it was a fact that it had never had such a busy year as the past one, never had so many cases been treated, and never before had we realized its inadequacy. It was not large enough for the wants of the tewn, and he therefore urged the congregation to do their best to help such a noble institution. At Wycliffe Church, the Rev. JOHN YoNon appealed for a more liberal contribution than in former years to i the funds of the Hospital, as it was n need of increased su opprt. Preashing at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday evening, the Rev. C. HARVEY COOK said that according to the ethics of some folks at that present day, the time to give a man a thrust was when he was down; but accord- ing to more humanitarian ethics,when t). man wa.l down, then was the time to give him a helping ihand. It was this sentiment which was in their hearts that day as they made an offering to the Warrington Infirmary. There was much to depress and sadden one in their common, every-day life, but there was also much to cheer and hearten them. The fact that a working men's committee, by their good organization, had secured nearly £l,OOO for a local healing institution was some- thing to be proud of. He was profoundly thankful that the working people had done so nobly for an institution that was doing noble work. They were queer minister- ing angels, some of them—sooty, hard-handed, hard- headed, fond of a glass, working hard, living, hard, yet sparing a thought for the poor folks who were down on their luck. However, it was not easy to show how much the men and women workers of the town had contributed to making the needy. We saw that they had given £l,OOO, but we did net see how much they had taken off this gift, by spending .money on things which were causing some of the very ills they had given their hind- earned money to cure. It was splendid of them to give as tbey had done, but what was the use of building up 11 with one hand, and pulling down with the other?

"WAS WORST IN SPRING !" A Worker's Startling Story.

IRRITATING ERUPTIONS ENDED BY ZAM-RUK.

All who are sorely troubled with skin eruptions at this season should lay t 9 heart the personal expfirience. of Mr. Frederick Perry, cabinet maker, of 45, Pritchard Street, Birmingham. To a" Sunday Sun "reporter Mr: Perry said :—" My trouble began when I was a boy ot 14. I had a break- ing out all over my face, and the doctor put it down to my system being poisoned. After a course of medicine and external applications tke disease seemed to die away but from that time until a few months ago I have always had these periodical outbreaks on the skin. At any time that I caught the least cold, or my blood got out of order, I would have the fearful breaking-out OD my face. It got so objectionable that I had to stay away from the works, as my fellow-shopmates used to object to my appearance. "Springtime used to be the most trying season for me, as then the disease was at its worst. I used to. suffer untold agonies from burning and irritation. At different times I bought all sorts of salves and oint- ments, none of which, however, effected a cure. This state of affairs continued, on and off, for over thirty years. Now lam in a position to declare that my face is completely cured through the use of Zarn-Buk. "After the first application of Zara-Buk, the burning sensation disappeared. I persevered, and after four or five dressings the scabs came off my face just like one great plaster, and underneath was perfectly dry and healthy skin. The skin continued to improve, and after I had used thres boxes I had obtained a perfect cure. Since Zam-Buk first healed my face I have never had the least sign of an eruption." The skin cannot remain unhealthy long with Zam- Buk. Zarn-Buk expels disease, puts the tissues into a healthy condition, and is thus unequalled for all erup- tions, pimples, blackheads, ringworm, poisoned or festering sores, eczema, psoriasis, ulcers, piles, bad legs, diseased ankles, sore backs, scurvy, dandruff, scalp irritation, barber's rash, abscesses, boils, cuts, bruises, burns, scalds, sprains, sore feet, neuralgia and nerve pains, colds in the head and on the chest, and all inflammations and diseases of the skin. Warrington v. Hunslet.

There was a good gate of about 5,000 people at War- rington on Saturday when Hunslet paid their return visit. Warrington having won the earlier engagement, the visitors came with the intention of avenging the defeat if possible. They were not quite at full strength, Place, C. Ward, and Whiteley being away, while A. Goldthorpe was compelled to take up the full back position. Warrington were as selected, with the excep- tion that Fish was an absentee and Berry came into the threequarter line again. The weather was fine and the ground in good order for a fast game. Teams : Warrington.—Hallam, back : Kenyon, Isherwood, Dickinson and Berry, three-quarter backs; D. WARRINGTON v. BATLEY.

Warrington brought off their postponed fixture with Batley at Batley on Tuesday. Fish, Boardman' and Naylor were absentees, while Batley had a strong team I up. The homesters were keen on winning, owing to their precarious position in the league, but the Warring- ton men were inclined to rc,›erve themselves for the cup-tie with Wigan. Wretched weather prevailed, and the ground was little better than a quagmire while the gate was the smallest of the season. Teams Batley.—Turton, back; W. P. Davies, Abbott, W. T. Davies, and Johnson, three-quarter backs ; Oakland and Bogg, half backs ; Fozzard, Clayton, Judge, Phillips, Wade, Tebbutt, Wolstenholme, and Hodgson' forwards. Warrington.—Hallam, back; Kenyon, Isherwood, Dickinson, and Berry, three-quarter backs ; D. Davies and Brooks, half backs; Preston, Boardman, Shugars, Thomas, Cook, Coleman, Swift, and Jolley, forwards. Referee, Mr. Kenne ly, Broughton. Touch judges, Mr. H. Sewell, Leeds, and Mr. P. F. Farrar, Halifax. Warrington had first advantage of a strong wind, but I despite this the home side were the first to score. Hallam kicked on his own line, with the result that Judge charged the ball, and scored between the posts, Oakland kicking a goal. After this Warrington reached the other end, where Isherwood was only just prevented from getting through after a strong run. The home men had to defend for a while, and then the forwards broke away in a body to the centre. They got no further, however, and for the rest of the first half the visitors were the attacking party, though they came nothing near scorirg. At the interval Batloy led by one goal and one try (5 points) to Warrington nil. ' Without the usual rest the teams crossed over, and for a long time play was of a scrambling character on a very muddy turf. Oakland just failed to cross, and then Will Davies dropped a goal, Amid a torrent of rain the visiting front rank carried play right to the Batley line, and had the hardest luck in not bringing about a score. Brooks was held under the posts, while Kenyon missed a pass on the line. Warrington attacked strongly with- out result. The game ended in Batley's favour by 2 goals and 1 try (7 points) to Warrington nil.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King is having numerous alteiations carried out in the C.stle and grounds at Windsor. The State apart- ments are also in the workmen's hands, and are being prepared for the King of Spain, who is expected at the beginning of June. The young King is exceedingly fond of horsss, and will probably be present at the fashionable race meeting at Ascot. Lately he has developed a craze for motoring, much to the dismay of his own police, and subject, who regard such an innova- tion as rather out of keeping with the traditions of the Spanish Royal Family. Don Alfonso is tall, rather thin, with a prominent r• ides' 0, and is impulsive and , generous in character. He is exceedingly wealthy, even I tor a monarch, as the riches of the Spanish Bourbons in land, jewels. and artistic treasures, are almost with- out equal in Europe. Another Royal guest who will also shortly pay his first visit to the Court of St. James is Prince Gustavus Adolphus of Sweden, to whom the charming Princess Margaret of Connaught is betrothed. I The Prince, who will receive a very hearty welcome in • this country is expected the end of next month, and his ' marriage with Princess Margaret will take place early in July, either in Lo idon, or at Wind-or. Princess Margaret's Future Home.—Princess Marge ret of Connaught, like most royalties, is an accomplished linguist, but she is not very familiar with Swedish, and is therefore now engaged in perrecting herself in that language. This is the nave necessary as the Queen and Crown Princess of Sweden are both more or less invalids, and a large number of social engagements are therefore likely to fall to de lot of Princess Margaret. For this reason she will probably not be able to spend so much time in this country after her marriage, as does her cousin Princess Charles of Denmark. It is a somewhat severe and simple home to which she is going. Stock- holm is a beautiful city, but the Royal Palace is a plian building with no pretensions of grandeur either inside Or out, and the tastes and habits of the Royal family are exceedingly quiet and sedate. These characteristics are also shared by the Swedish people. It is a land of Peasant industries, in which lace-making and the cottage handloom play an important part, and in Sweden practi- cally every woman is skilled in the art of needlework. Afternoon Tea on the Terrace.—Mr. Winston Churchill, M.P., is understood to entertain grist politi- cal ambitions, and it seems that he objects to the House of Commons being made a place of social entertainments. 'Of late years, afternoon tea on the Terrace has become a popular attraction for fashionable society, during the Parliamentary summer. Many members are only too glad to wile away some of their weary hours of their enforced attendance at Westminster, by entertaining their lady friends to tea and strawberries on the Terrace, sPending the time more agreeably in the fresh air,whilst Society women, even if a life of pleasure is the sole Object of existence, are nothing loth to make us of an oPportunity to affect a passing acquaintancs with politi- cal problems. Some months ago it was announced that the Kitchen Committee had decided not to enter into the usual contract for the supply of summer straw- berries, owing to the possibility of an early dissolution of Parliament, hut it is now thought that the Session will run its normal course, and, in that case, there will be the usual social gatherings on the Terrace, in spite of Mr. Winston Churchill's opinion that the House of Commons should be a place of business only. Liberal Hostesses.—The Liberals have suffered a great loss by the death of Lady Tweedmouth, and Lady Spencer, two of the leading political hostesses who used to entertain for their party, during the Parliamentary season. At the best, few of the great houses in London, Wits spacious suites of rooms are available for the Liberals. Those possessing such mansions, are mostly Unionists, and though the political Salon, as it existed in the days of Lady Palmerston has largely ceased to existaet these social entertainments are not without their influence upon Party movements and prospects. On behalf of the Liberals, Lady Hayter, has thrown Open her splendid house in Grosvenor Square, and on the same side figures LadyDenman,one of the most youth ful of political hostesses. Indifferent health has hitherto kept Lady Campbell-Bannerman from doing much enter- taining, but she has given one or two receptions this season, and the obligations of Liberals are due also, in the matter of party entertaining to Lady Aberdeen and Mrs. Asquith. Children are Fashionable.—Since the Queen adopted the practice of driving in the park with one or other of her little grandchildren, the inmates of the nursery have become quite fashionable. They are in requisition in the drawing room, and for the afternoon drive, whether they like it or not. Probably they mostly do like it, as there are few children who do not appreciate being read° a fuss of oven if they are required to be on their best behaviour. If anyone objects it is probably their rilarses, as, though it sounds very extraordinary to say • sO, there are many nurses in the service of fashionable society, who are afraid to entrust their young charges With their mothers. Poisonous Dyes.—Our attention is once more directed 1.3 Y the medical Press, to the dangers of poisonous dyes In dress materials. Fifty per cent of the cheaper kinds Of hosiery we are told, contain poisonous products in appreciable quantities, and it is stated that the public anislyst at Nottingham extracted half a grain of arsenic from one pair of stockings. This is more than enough cause fatal blood-poisoning. Thepresence of arsenic In any garments is bad enough, but in clothing worn Ilext the skin, it is very dangerous, and in undercloth- ing it is best, as far as possible, to wear only the natural tuadyed articles. Stockings of the highly colored I,vprieties should he avoided always, and the better kinds that can be relied upon as being vegetable dyed re in the end the cheaper and safer purchase. Lace and Net.—Lace blouses are greatly seen, and the tference at the moment is apparently for silk Spanish e either in black or cream. This is invariably Illounted upon chiffon of the same hue, and made up "er Bain to match. Net, spotted or plain, is also being Much utilised for dressy blouses, the net in all cases !etching in colour the skirt with which the blouse is :Yens. Net again is being used by the furlong for the 4?w neck ruffles, which have "caught on" tremendously. These becoming little accessories are all round, and smaller than the ruffles of a few sc,sons back, which frequently reached higher than the ears, and imparted a Muffled up effect. There is no denying the fact how- eyes that the net ruffle is an expensive little luxury. Even when built at home by clever dainty fingers, the cost is considerable and the first shower of rain, or even a.little mist, spells ruin to it. Under the most favourable ciorteeumthse cie circumstances ruffle s the net rue is short lived, but when we rt g'htful softness it imparts, and its general hecomingness, it is not to be greatly marvelled at, that We forget its costliness and lack of durability. , About Muffs.—Although the muff destined to be simply useful has now been careful y packed away, the Tuff ornamental is still well to the fore. Hats and ;Stuffs match, being generally in chiffon sad lace, dark hats and muffs being worn with white, cream, or light stumes, and vice versa. Unquestionably the muff possesses great decorative value, also it has a happy icpack of causing its wearer to appear at her best, as it Eves her something to do with her hands. For motor- sog the; new muffs of fluffy rabbit wool, kaitted up to ol.t like fluffy fur, are charming, but they will be found to hold the dust much more than the close furs, genes- '4l3, set aside for this delightful means of locomotion, and are therefore not likely to enjoy a great vogue. _saDainty Accessorics.—Theatre bags, to hold opera a ruses, fan and handkerchief are necessities to smart &ler, and :these cla*nty little items are now being rairro;vith several tiny inner packets which bold a P a powder puff, a bag of rir 3 powder, a box of salve' and a glove buttoner. MOTOR NOTES.

It is rather odd to find how widely the opinions of motorists differ in respect to the value of a motor car after it has been in use for some little while assure_ ing that during that time it has bean properly while, to and carefully driven. In an article recently appear - ing in the "Daily Chronicle" from the pen of Mr. Henry Norman, the writer asserted that a car, which when new cost £lB5, should, after five years' reasonable use, "be a bargain at £85."

In complete contiast to this optimistic view, a gentle- man, signing himself "Private Owner," contributes his own experience when disposing of good second-hand cars. "Last /ear," he says, " I sold al2 h.p. car, the original cost of which was £425, for £32s,the car having b. -n used less than 6 months, and not having run more than 3.000 miles. Included in the price were extras and spares ce-ting more then. £30."

"The other day," he continues," I sold a car costing £550 last July for £4OO. In this case the car had not run 700 miles, and was practically as good as new. In both instancos the cars were of well-known makes. It was only after advertising for wE3ks in the best of the automobile jouinals that I effected a sale at all." In this case it will be seen that the depreciation for a period of less than nine months worked out at £l5O, or at the rate of £2OO pr annum, which is certainly a sum calculated to deter even the most enthusiastic novice from embarking on the pastime on h;s own account.

As a matter of fact, we feel bound to consider that both Mr. Henry Norman's hopeful view and a" Private Owner's" depressing experience are extreme. If the car belonging to the latter gentleman had really been moderately and carefully driven throughout the shori. time it was in his possession ; if it had sustained no injury through collision or other accident, and if the motor its3lf • were proved to be in thoroughly good working order, then there is no doubt that it should have realised a much better price than was obtained. On the other hand, there are a number of motorists who, being either careless or peculiarly unfortunate, meet with an almost incredible number of accidents in quite a short time, in the remedying of which the outlay of a considerable sum of money is imperative. The cash so expended they are apt to re- gard—when the moment for selling arrives—in the light of an investment made for the improvement of the car and to add it accordingly to the original price paid, whilst, as a matter of fact, there is no doubt that every accident really means far more damage done than is es:imated in the subsequent repairs.

But we are afraid that the idea of a car costing £lB5 when new, running for five years, during which time it covers a fair amount of mileage, and then selling "as a bargain" at £B5, is somewhat far-fetched, Think for a moment of the cars that were in vogue five years ago, and ask yourself how many of them—even though they cost mall more than the sum names—anyone would care to be seen driving now? And there is little doubt that, five years hence, the vehicles which now appeal to us as nearly perfect, from a constructional point of view will then be old-fashioned, and, even in some cases, obselete patterns. Moreover, the prices of cars are coming down and they will doubtless be lower still, later on; so that, all things considered, the chances seem to be dead against a motorist finding a man willing to pay almost half the original price for a second-hand car which is hopelessly old-fashioned and has had five years steady wear.

HOW GIANT MACHNOW KEEPS HEALTHY.

Machnow, the Russian Giant, for some little time past has been the centre of attraction in London, and his remarkable appetite, no less than his enormous pro- portion in height sod weight, has created a sensation. He is not a gourmand, but his daily bill of fare in quantity and quality is enough to cause astonishment. About two quarts of milk or tea, 16 hard boiled eggs, and six to eight small loaves and butter provide the giant with a morning repast; whilst a luncheon at noon consists of from two to thrc3 pounds of meat, five pound of potatoes and a quart of beer. For dinner, he has soup, three to live pounds of meat, fowl, fish, vegetables, potatoes, three pound of bread, and one to two quarts of beer. The day is closed with a supper of 10 to 15 eggs, with bread and butter, and one quart of tea. There are also a few "extras " in the way of refreshments. To partake of such a vast quantity of food without experiencing ill-effects therefrom might be regarded as marvellous, but Machnow has been generous enough to unfold the secret of his wonderful digestive powers. Through his Manager, the giant has just written a letter to the proprietors of Bile Beans, the well-known vegetable specific, stating that after his customary heavy meals he finds Bile Beans to be an excellent aid to digestion and a safe and valuable aperient medicine. Testimony'such as this will,no doubt, prove highly satisfactory to the proprietors of this world-famed remedy.

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

THE PRISON TRII3LTTE.

Lady Battersea told in a lecture at Westminster the other night that when visiting Aylesbury Prison a few days after the death of Queen Victoria she noticed several of the convicts wearing little black bows on their dresses. She inquired the reason, and was told that it was in memory of the late Queen. The bows had been fashioned out of little scraps of material the convicts had pulled out of their boot- laces.

SOCIET Y LINGUISTS.

According to .71.A.P. there are many well-known women who speak and write a number of foreign languages. Lady Mary Fitzwilliam knows eleven languages, and is said to be learning Persian. Lady Ela Russell, a sister of the Duke of Bedford, also has the gift of tongues, and once began to learn Chinese. Lady Griffin, wife of Sir Lepel Griffin, speaks four languages perfectly; Lady Egerton, wife of Sir Edwin Egerton—now Ambassador at Rome—is another excellent linguist; and Princess Blucher, who, although not English, is well known in London, speaks five languages fluently—English, French, German, Italian, and Spanish.

SPRING TOILET.

Lent has given the shops a splendid eximse for dressing their windows in dreams of mauve, from the richest royal purple to the dainty, faint tint that is seen in the lilac sprays used for decorative purposes. Our sketch shews an original spring toilet carried out in various shades of violet. The material chosen for its realisation is a very fine souple cloth. The skirt is absolutely plain, hanging in rich, simple folds; the bodice is much

cut away in front, bordered with dark violet velvet, and fastened with the new tinted pearl buttons, and it opens over a crossover of pale violet chiffon, which in its turn opens over a vest of white Irish crochet. The puff sleeves are slightly rucked, and are finished with gauntlet cuffs at the elbow, softened with tiny frills of knife-pleated chiffon. A dainty little French chapeau in pale violet lace straw, trimmed with wreaths of shaded flowers, is worn with this costume.

GOSSIP.

Who is responsible, asks the World, for the theory pervading so many dramas, that noble ladies sit round in rows after dinner and say waspish things to one another, chiefly on the score of their ages and complexions? The modern author is almost invariably a prey to a series of banal hallucinations that beset his inkpot with the immobility of an invincible obsession. BOLERO CAPE.

A useful little wrap is sliewn in the sketch of a new bolero cape. It is carried out in a fawn cloth of light weight. The sleeveless bolero is made tight-fitting and is double-breasted, fastening on the left side with fancy green buttons. Over this is a double cape, the edges left unheinined, and orna- mented with a narrow black and white braid. The capes are stitched to the bolero a little way down the front, and are held together beneath a stitched

strap of tho material. At the back the straps meet in a point at the waist, the capes being drawn in at the waist by another short strap, adorned with buttons at each end. A turned-down collar of green velvet, having a lace motif placed at each corner, gives a smart finish to this becoming cape. The hat chews one of the newest styles of spring millineEy ; it is of brown straw, and turns up sharply behind, and it is trimmed with pink roses and foliage.

FASHIONS AND F.kNCIES.

In spite of Lent, there have been several functions in Loudon at which some very lovely frocks have been worn; and these garments are the forerunners of the fashions which will prevail during the season immediately following Easter.

Pale tints are prevalent for afternoon frocks, hydrangea mauves and blues being easily first, and a sort of pale milk-chocolate brown having also a very large share of popularity. At Signor Marconi's wedding a distinguished guest wore a dress of this colour trimmed with woollen lace of exactly the Caine shade, threaded with dark brown chenille. It was,very effective.

, Voiles and canvas, and light tweeds and cloths, are at present the most generally used materials, preceding the soft fabrics of the corning Season. The present vogue of full skirts, says a Globe writer, demands materials of the softest nature, in order that the voluminous folds may fall with grace and artistic fitness. Skirts, both long and short, are worn full and pleated, and therefore stilt materials are quite out of the question. The cult of the short skirt continues.

To-DA-'s RECIPE.

Rica Sour.—Few cooks make good soups, declares "Madge" in Truth. The reason is that they cook the ingredients too much. Try this one: rut into a stewpan a knuckle of veal, about 41b. of lean beef cut into chumps (not too small), and add onions, celery, carrots, parsnips, leeks, and parsley, and any lean ham or a ham bone if you have it. Add enough water to cover all. Let it gently stew for four hours. Stir it often, but do not touch the bottom, or the liquid will not be clear. Pass the whole through it sieve, and set it away until next day. Then remove every particle of fat, nave ready in a stewpan a lump of butter and an onion, and when this is quite hrowirpour in the soup. Let it just come tc the boil, and then add meat extract, wine, or catsup, anil poor I he soup through a calico bag into the soup tureen. Serve It with sippets of fried bread cut into dice-shaped pieces.

RATS IN EAST LONDON.

A Girl's Serious Fright.

There is a plague of rats in the East End of London. A girl named Louisa Randall,l2l,Randolph-road, Custom House, London, E., was lilting some boxes in a factory, when an enormous rat leaped out over her shoulder. When she got home her mother was startled by the terrible effect the shock had had upon her. She was a thin, anmmic girl, stunted in her development through want of blood. The fright caused St. Vitus' dance. For four months she was always spilling her food, dropping plates and dishes. When she walked she would throw one foot forwai d and drag the other, like a paralytic, and presently she was actually paralysed all down one side. An accidental wetting led to rheumatic fever. She seemed an invalid for life.

"But one day," continued Mrs. Randall, "I read of a girl who had suffered just as Louisa was suffering. We decided to try if Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, which cured this girl, would do good. After the third box of pills' Louisa could control her fingers. New blood began to flow in her veins. The paralysis and the pain went altogether. Soon Louisa got up, after three months in bed. " Auiemia had retarded her development, but with new blood she was soon a healthy girl. A little time ago she had three weeks at a convalescent home in Eastbourne, and that completed the magnificent cure which Dr. Williams' pink pills effected. My daughter hopes to begin business again soon, and she is certainly strong enough for anything." There can be no doubt as to the cure, Miss Randall looks now more like a country girl, than a Londoner. She has red, rosy cheeks, and clear bright eyes, which give evidence of her keen intelligence. For many girls (and young men too) Dr. Williams' pink pills have proved the turning point in development, changing weakly constitutions,threatened with decline and death, to sound, overflowing health, because they make new blood. Mothers know why they and their daughters need new blood more than men; but men can be anmmic' too. The weak back, easy fatigue, lack of interest in life, indigestion and general nervous weak- ness which trouble both men and women, need new blood to cure them, and these pills make new blood. Nerves that need new blood to feed them show their need in neuralgia, fits, St. Vitus' dance, eventually paralysis, locomotor ataxy and utter breakdown. But Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people can cure all these things—the genuine pills. It is important to avoid substitutes, and purchasers should take care to see the full name, as above, on the wrapper, before paying.

ST. HELENS.

COLLIERY FATALITY AT HAYDOCK.

The circumstances under which John Champion, aged fifty-one, of Vista-road, Haydock, was killed at the Princess Pit, Haydock, on Friday night, were inquired into on Tuesday night by Mr. Brighouse county coroner. Several witnesses were called as to thafalling in of the roof where Champion was working, but how the accident was caused was the subject of a long discussion. At last an additional witness, John Courtfield, said he saw deceased knock out one of the props and the 'roof at once fell id. Verdict, " Accidental death." „

BRUTALITY AT Sr. HELENS. John Leary, labourer, of 24. Bold-street, was prose- cuted at St. Helens, on Wednesday, by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children for cruelly neglecting his five children, who had been removed to the Workhouse.—Mr. E. W. Swift prosecu- ted, and called Inspector Hunter, who said that on the 20th inst. defendant's son came to the police office in a state of terror. Witness 'lent to the house, and found prisoner very drunk in a house some doors away. Three of the youngest children were alone in the house. They sheltered the boy at the police office all night.—John Leary, aged twelve, spoke to his father threatening him. He had seen his father kick a dog's teeth out in the house, and also kill a cat in the house with the poker. He told him he would do the same to him if he told anybody aboutithe cat.—Leary was sentenced to six months' hard labour.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

DEATH OF A RUNCORN BOATOWNER.

Mr. died suddenly on Sunday at the age of seventy-one years. The deceased was a boatowner and carrier, having an office in Liverpool, and he was well known in shipping circles. About twenty years ago he was a member of Runcorn Board of amprovement Commissioners, but since then he had taken no part in public affairs. MOTHER'S FATAL GRIEF._

At Ditton, near Widnes, on Tuesday, -Isabella Capel (45), a married woman, residing in Pitville Cottages, Ditton, dropped down dead on hearing of the death of her twelve-months-old daughter. She went out to work as laundress, at Hough Green, leaving her baby with a woman to nurse until she came home. The child was ill when she left, and on the way home she was met by a boy, who broke the news to her. Deceased hurried home, and, on seeing the child, exclaimed, " Oh, my child." Falling down, she was ' assisted to the sofa, where she expired. Her busband deserted her and six children some eighteen months ago, and these circum- stances, coupled with the sad demise render the affair most pitiful. The coroner has been notified, and an inquest will be held. RUNCORN PRINTERS AND THE FACTORY ACT.

At Runcorn, on Monday, .Messrs. R. Moore and Co.. Limited, printers, High-street, were summoned for employing a woman named Florence Robinson contrary to the Factories and Workshops Act. Mr. R. E. Graves, inspector of factories, said the woman was employed an hour and a half after hours.—Mr. H. N. Linaker, for the defence, said the firm was in liquidation, and be appeared for Hr. Voisey, of Warrington, as liquidator. He was instructed to plead guilty. Any penalty the magistrates inflicted would have to be paid by the credi- tors, and not by the firm.—Mr. Graves said a serious element in the case was that this was the fourth time Messrs. Moore had been before the court since 1902.—A fine of £1 and costs was imposed. RUNCORN CHEMIST AND HIS FAMILY. Hugh 111`Leod, described as ananalytical chemist, was summoned before the Runcorn magistrates on Monday for neglecting to provide reasonable maintenance for his wife Eva anJ their five children. The defendant did not appear. Mr. H. N. Linaker, for the complain- ant, said the parties were married in 1888, and there had been eleven children, of whom five were living. The defendant was an analytical chemist, and could earn good wages. He had not worked since last October. Mrs. WLeod said she now lived at 7, Picow-street, Runcorn, and she was being supported by her father and mother. Her husband hadnot contributed anything to her support since the beginning of November. The Lench granted Mrs. IWLeod a separation order with custody of the children and £1 per week maintenance.

HOCKEY. ,

.3BIRKENHEAD PARK 2ND V. WARRINGTON ROYAL.,

Played at Warrington, the Park being victorious by three goals to two. Warrington were the first to score through Smith, but the visitors soon drew level through Edwards, quickly followed by a beautiful goal from the stickof D. L. Roberts. Play was evenly contested up to half-time, and Bates put on a third goal for the Park. In the second half each side pressed in turn, and Warrington were successful in scoring again through their centre forward.

UNDERGROUND TELEGRAPH WIRE.

The pipes for the underground telegraph line from London to Glasgow are now practicAlly completed, and the permanent cable has been laid between (London and Manchester and Liverpool. A temporary cable has been drawn into the pipes between Manchester and Bradford and Leeds. The permanent cable has also been drawn in between Warrington and Carlisle, but it will not be ready for use for two or three months.

EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

ELECTION OF CHAIRMAN AND VICE-CHAIRMAN.

Mr. Henry Roberts elected a me*mber of the Committee.

Mr. F. MONKS, J.P. having resigned the first duty of the members of the Warrington Education Committee at their annual meeting on Wednesday evening was to appoint a successor. Pending the selection of a chair- man Councillor Burton presided over the deliberations of the committee expressing his regret, which was echoed by those present, at Mr. Monks having found it necessary to retire. Alderman Bennett has acted as the deputy to Mr. Monks since the formation of the Educa- tion authority and the committee were unhesitating andunanimous in appointing him to the more important office.

Councillor EVANS said they all knew Alderman Bennett was deeply interested in educational matters and that he would give himself heart and soul to the work of the committee. The best of his abilities would be brought to bear upon the work. In Alderman Bennett he thought they would have an excellent chairman. (Hear, hear.) Councillor WILKINSON seconded, the motion being, as above stated, approved. Alderman BENNETT thanked the committee very warmly for the honour they had conferred upon him. To follow in the chair a man so capable and public spirited as Mr. F. Monks was no light task. They were all united in regretting Mr. Monks' retirement, especially the cause of it, and in wishing him a tranquil sunset to a long, strenuous and useful life. He (the speaker) was conscious that there were other members of the committee with much greater experience in matters educational than any to which he could lay claim and who would have more worthily occupied the chair. It was however felt that the position should be held, except in special circumstances, by a member of the ' Town Council, and the lot had fallen upon him. He had not sought the position and was sensible alike of its responsibilities and of his defects, but he knew the kindness of the members of the committee and could only say he would try not to disappoint them. They had hitherto been singularly free from anything which savoured of party bias or sectarian feeling. He would endeavour to preserve that high tradition and to hold the scales impartially. He trusted they would continue to work harmoniously together, sinking all minor differences of politics or creed, and striving only for the welfare of the children and the best and brightest interest of the town. (Applause.) APPOINTMENT OF VICE-CHAIRMAN. QUESTION OF PRINCIPLE RAISED.

Councillor HULSE moved and Alderman SMETHURST seconded the election of Alderman Pierpoint as vice- chairman of the Committee.

Councillor CRAIK moved an amendment upon a ques- tion of principle as he failed to see that both the chair- man and deputy-chairman should be members of the Town Council, in fact he thought it rather a hindrance than otherwise. He submitted for consideration the name of the Rev. Morley Stevenson. His nominee had, he said, served as chairman of the most important sub- committee—the school attendance committee—and from the way he had introduced the minutes of the sub-comMittee he was sure every member of the com- mittee would feel that in him they had a gentleman of the highest standing and one in whom they had unbounded confidence.

Councillor WILKINSON seconded the amendment. The Rev. MoitLEr STEVENSON however declined to allow his nomination to stand. He felt very grateful to the gentlemen who had submitted his name. He however felt that in business matters they would be better under the guidance of Alderman Pierpoint. Councillor LORD supported the nomination of Alder- man Pierpoint. His principle reason for doing so was that by adopting the original motion they might induce Alderman Pierpoint to continue his membership of the committee in spite of his expressed intensiod of retiring. The Rev. JOHN YONGE did not oppose the motion from any personal reason, but because he had a strong con- viction that all talents did not lie in the members of the Town Council but that on the Education Committee they had gentlemen who were equal in ability and in the interest they displayed in educational matters. In his mind it was very desirable that the honours,if there were any, should be divided between Town Council and co-opted representatives. (Hear, hear.) In Mr. F. Monks they bad a co-opted member who made an exceed- ingly able chairman. (Flear, hear.) He had been s acceded bya member of the Town Council and he (the speaker) thought it would be a very happy arrangement for a co-opted member to be appointed to the vice-chairman. He felt strongly upon a matter of principle—all the honours should not be given to members of the Town Council, and therefore moved that Mr. C. E. Parker be elected to the office of vice-chairman. _

Mr. PARKER also declined to allow himself to go to the vote, as, he said, he felt he would be incompetent. He had so many other duties that,although he was much obliged to Mr. Yonge, he could not agree to allow him- self to be nominated. _

No other nominations were forthcoming and Alder- man Pierpoint was unanimously elected. In returning thanks he said he had not sought the position, he never wanted it and was sorry they had elected him to fill it. The members of the Corporation were fully alive to the abilities of the co-opted mem- bers of the committee. Personally he felt he had a great deal to learn in the matter of education and he would ask for the kindly consideration and help from every member of the Committee, which he felt he roquired. He was sorry to have been opposed to his friend the Rev. Morley Stevenson, and whether the decision of the Committee was for the good of the educational system of the borough remained to be seen. ELECTION OF SUB-COMMITTEES. THE ATTENDANCES OF MENIBERS.

The Committee next proceeded to elect the various sub-committees, and in not one single instance was the original proposition passed with comment and motions to amend. The composition of the:Finance Committee as proposed by Mr. A. J. Jolley, seconded by Councillor Wilkinson, was :—Aldermen Fairclough and Alderman Pierpoint, Councillors Sankey, Tinnion and Wilkinson, Messrs. J. D. Buckton, R. T. Fairclough, A. J. Jolley (chairman), H. Kingdon, and W. Bibby. Mr. Bibby was proposed as the 'successor to Mr. C. E. Parker who retired from the committee. _ _ . .

Councillor LORD led the opposition to the motion. In looking at the attendanzes of some of the gentle- men, he said, he found some were very bad. Last year Alderman Pairclough only attended one meeting out of fifteen, and during two years he had attended three meetings out of 27, Alderman Pierpoint had attended badly and Councillor Tinnion had only attended three meetings out of twelve. He thought they should elect as members of sub-committees only gentlemen who were prepared to do the work. (Hear, hear.) If those gentlemen who did not attend to the duties had not the courtesy to retire he was prepared to nominate others to take their places, He had pleasure in proposing that Mr, May be made a member of the committee. Councillor ELLISON seconded. Alderman BENNETT in answer to Mr. Buckton said they had the power to increase the number of members of the committee. He however thought it a mistake to have too large a committee. The REV. 30IIN YONGE : The Committee is made less by the members staying away. (Hear, hear). Mr. WALKER agreed with Councillor Lord. He thought it useless to re-appoint men whe did not attend to the'duties. He thought the size of the committee should be increased as some of the members did not turn up and do the work. (Hear, hear). Alderman BENNETT : It is desirable that Alc:erman Fairclough should be a member of the committee as he is [the chairman of the finance committee of the Corporation. Mr. PLINSTON : It is desirable Alderman Fairclough should attend to the duties appertaining to the office. With all due deference to Alderman Fauslough I do not think it right he should occupy a position and never discharge the duties of that position. Other members are willing to take part and do useful work and if Alderman Fairclough or anyone else has not done his duty his name should not be retained. Alderman BENNETT : I think it will be a mistake to strike his name off.

Alderman PIERPOINT : Alderman Fairelough is a member of five committees of th 3 Corporation and does his fair share of the work of the Corporation if not of the Education Committee. _ .

Alderman BENNETT : In the case of any emergency it is important Alderman Fairelough should attend the deliberations of the Finance Committee.

Councillor WHArcsos: Alderman Fairclough is in his right place whether he attends or not. He is there to be called upon when he is wanted. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY thought it was not desirable that they should alter the constitution of the committee. The Finance Committee should not have a preponder- ance of co-opted members, and if the amendments were carried and Mr. May's name substituted for one of the Council members, co-opted members would be in the majority. Alderman BENNETT : Alderman Pierpoint is an ex-officio member of the committee so there is room for both Mr. Bibby and Mr. May. Councillor EVANS moved the election of Councillor Fulton Smith. Councillor FULTON Smrrii however, declined to allow the nomination to stand. . _ .

Upon a vote being taken the original motion was carried.

The Rev. MORLEY STEVENSON pointed out that a vacancy was caused on this committee by the death of Alderman Sutton. He thought however, that the filling of that vacancy should be left over. He proposed that Mr. Bnckton be elected a member of the Committee. He would not press his motion if he bad an assurance that Mr. Buckton's name would be con- sidered for the first vacancy. Councillor LORD seconded.

Councillor EVANS :—Th—O;e; is at present a vacancy. Alderman PIERPOINT said that on the Committee there were already four representatives of the Church. Mr. li. T. FAIRCLOUGII : Not of the Church ; of the Church schools.

Alderman PIERPOINT : To my mind they represent the Church, uhatever people may say. Upon a vote being taken the election of Mr. Buckton was deferred, the committee being constituted as proposed by the Rev. Morley Stevenson. Councillor ELLISON moved that the School Attend- ance Committee be composed of the following gentle- men, Councillors Barton. Brough, Ellison, Evans, Lord and Sankey, Messrs. William Bibby, Thomas May, Edwin Plinston and George Walker. Councillor BROUGH seconded. Mr. UNswourff asked why the name of Councillor Maginn had been left off the committee and was informed that Councillor Magian had not attended a single meeting. Councillor CIWK moved that Mr. Upson be appointed a member of the committee. No one grumbled more than Mr. Upson about the school attendance. Mr. UesoN protested that he did not grumble, he I merely criticised. If he was appointed the causes for I grumbling might be removed.
