MR. MONKS'S RESIGNATION.

The director read the following letter which had been addressed by Mr. F. Monks to the Town Clerk :—On resigning my position in the Education Committee of the County Borough, I wish to express my thanks to the Council for the honour they have done me in elect- ing me to represent the Technical Education Com- mittee with which I was so long connected. I regret I feel I cannot any longer with justice to myself con- tinue the work which requires a large amount of thought and time if the work is to be done satisfactorily in the interests of the town and education particu- larly. Having put in more than fifty years of what I call public service, lam sure you will agree with me that I urn entitled to take things more easily for the few remaining years that may be left to me. Yours faithfully, F. MONKS. The CHAIRMAN said he was sure he was expressing the feelings of the committee when he said they received the intimation with the greatest possible regret. It would be impossible to exaggerate Mr. Monks's services to the town and the feeling was absolutely universal both in the committee and in the town generally of deep regret that he had been obliged to take that step. He had been of incalculable service to the committee in its earlier stages and his work on behalf of the town was really beyond all praise. Men of the type of Mr. Honks were the salt of the earth and happy was the community which could boast such citizens. They hoped he would soon be restored to perfect health and live amongst them as an honourable and useful citizen for many years to come. (Hear, hear.) MR. HENRY ROBERTS ELECTED A MEMBER The concluding business of the meeting, which gave rise to some discussion, was "To recommend to the Town Council a person of experience in Education, or acquainted with the needs of the various schoolslin the Boiough, for appointment as a selected member of this committee." This step was necessitated by the resigna- tion of Mr. F. Monks. Mr. J. D. BUCKTON moved that Mr. Charles Sawkins Burgess be recommended for appointment, remarking that that gentleman fulfilled the requirements as set forth above. He had not only been a manager but had acted as treasurer of one school for many years, and the Education Committee had already shown confidence in him by appointing him a representative manager of People's College. He had lived in Warringtru all his life, and was well known and respected. He always attended well to any duties ,he undertook, and he (Mr. Buckton) believed the Com- mittee would never regret the appointment. Alderman SMETHURST seconded. Councillor Craik was of the opinion that the Corn- mittee could not fill Mr. Monks's place more worthily than by appointing Mr. Henry Roberts a member. He did not know of anyone in the borough who knew more about the educational needs of Warrington, and who had done more in his time for educational work than Mr. Roberts. He was thoroughly capable of fulfilling the duties in every detail, and he (Councillor Craik) moved that he be recommended for appointment. The Rev. JOHN YONdE said he was about to rise for the same purposefas Councillor Craik. He felt sure that Mr. Henry Roberts would serve the committee admir- ably. He was an exceedingly able man, was in full sympathy with all educational movements, and would greatly assist the committee in their various debates. He had particular talents which fitted him for the post and he (Mr. Yonge) was exceedingly glad Councillor Craik proposed his name. He could make a speech about Mr. Henry Roberts if it were necessary, but he did not think it was. He was a personal friend of his (Mr. Yonge's) he was thankful to say, and he (Mr. Yonge) was all the more glad, in consequence, to be in the Position of seconding the nomination with all possible heartiness. Mr. J. UPSON said he rose with very great reluctance to propose anothername. He had not a word to say against either of the gentlemen who had been proposed but he was sure those who had had experience of the gentleman whose name he was going to submit would acknowledge that he had a superior acquaintance with educational matters. He would submit the name of Mr. F. W. Monks, believing that the greatest compli- ment they could pay to the chairman who had presided over them for two years would be Ito elect his son a member of the committee. Those who worked on the School Management committee during the first twelve months knew how admirably, in the difficult circumstances of putting the Act in operation in Warrington. Mr. Monks guided the committee, and how great was his knowledge of education in all its branches. It was rather a painful thing to submit one name in opposition to two others, but he thought the educational claim of Mr. Monks were in this particular line superior to those of the other two. Councillor HULSE seconded.

Councillor WILKINSON : Has Mr. Upson any reason to think that Mr. Monks would accept the office. lam inclined to think he would rather remain in retirement.

Mr. UPSON: I don't think it is fair iiaTis;il'oZi—be asked that question. It has not been asked whether the other two would serve.

The CHAIRMAN: I was going to ask it in regard to each of them. _

Mr. UPSON: lam not in a position to say whether iMr. Monks would stand and I don't think that should ' 'lllu/ince us in voting unless we know from the other two gentlemen whether they are prepared to accept. Councillor CRAIK pointed out that Mr. F. W. Monks, had already been a member of the committee and I resigned; whereas the others had not had an oppor- tunity of serving on it. Mr. UPSON : Mr. Monks did not resign his position. He automEqically ceased to be a member of the com- mittee because he resigned from the Council. Councillor BROCIGII : I have reason to believe that Mr. Roberts will stand if elected. Councillor WILKINSON: I have more than a reason to believe that he will.

A vote was then taken and the figures were :—Mr. Henry Roberts, 15 ; Mr. F. W. Monks, 7 ; Mr. C. S. Burgess, 4. Mr. ROBERTS was than unanimously recemmended for election.

THE 14',XAMINER.

APRIL 1. 1905.

THE PARLIAMEN- TARY FARCE.

The Parliamentary farce, which will

always be associated

with Mr. BALFOUR'S , Premiership, was enacted again on Tuesday night. Mr. Josnru WALrox, in moving his resolution, said that the House of Commons could not be put off with the plea that it had nothing to do with the subject of Retaliation. It had a right to call on Mr. BALFOUR to explain how he proposed to deal with hostile tariffs. This was like calling spirits from the vast deep. We do not believe that Mr. BALFOUR has the slightest intention of explaining, even at the General Election. Ho has laid down the prin ziple that we ought to retaliate on the countries, which impose heavy tariffs on our goods. It is a principle which cannot be explained except by an adminis- trative Act applied to a particular case. If the constituencies were green enough to give Mr. BALFOTJR a mandate they would wake up one morning and find a heavy duty clapped on some necessary commodity, which would promptly I advance in price. The late Lord SALISBURY, who was in favour of Retaliation of a sort, never explained it beyond the point where Mr. BALFOUR has taken it up. it might be necessary said Lord SALISBURY, to deny certain foreign goods access to , our markets until the country in which they I were produced bad consented to moderate It 3 tariff. But what country and what goods should be selected for the retaliatory process Lord SALTS- BuitY never would tell, and Mr. BALFOUR will not tell. There is, of courso, only one way of working with a policy which cannot be explained until it explains itself with actions. Debating it in the House of Commons is of small service when there is nothing to go upon but the blessed formula, and when its supporters would rather not go into the Division Lobby. Mr. BALFoim and his policy wil

be rejected at the polls as soon as we can get to the polls. We shall kuow just as much about Retaliation then as ;vs know now. But the knowledge will be amply sufficient. Every- body can see that the movement to abandon free imports for the sake of retaliating on some foreign tariff will start you on an adventure which leads straight to Mr. CHAMBERLALN'S proposals. It is simply die to imagine that you can play with retaliatory duties without strengthening the case for differential duties in favour of the Colonies. As nobody would hear in any case of retaliating on our Protectionist-Colonies, the retaliation on foreign countries would at once establish a Colonial Preference. Mr. BALFOUR must be perfectly alive to this, although he is careful not to say so. On Wednesday, Mr. BALFOUR treated the House of Commons in his characteristic fashion. He professed to be quite unaware that on the previous night the House had condemned his fiscal policy by a resolution against which he and his party were afraid to divide. having read the resolution with ostentatious indifference, he announced that he saw no reason for taking any action. Mr. CHURCHILL asked whether such a decision was consistent with public duty and personal honour, but Mr. BatFoun was quite unmoved. On the point of honour, it is refreshing to know the opinion of the "Daily Telegraph," which informs the Opposition that they are not waging" honour- able warfare " ; they have violated the "Parlia- mentary armistice": " they will take cover under a. white flag or fire upon one with sublime impar- tiality." As the "Daily Telegraph "likened the Liberal party lately to "hungry wild beasts at the Zoo," the new impeachment seemed a trifle flat. But what is the "armistice " which the Opposition have disregarded? Merely because Mr. BALFOUR holds that the present Parliament has nothing to do with the fiscal question, the Opposition are expected to consider themselves bound by that opinion, and they continue their attacks. This is not "honourable warfare " ! Teesday's resolution, entered in the journals of the House without a dissentient voice, is beneath Mr. BALFOUR'S notice. Everything that happens in the House when he chooses to absent himself is beneath his notice. He does not read the news- papers. He does not even ask a private secretary what the House has been doing; but if a vote of No Confidence should be moved from the Front Opposition Bench, Mr. BALFOUR will condescend to accept the challenge.

Does the Prime Minister still command the confidence of his party Some of them suggest that he does not. The Tariff Reform league has scoffed at his fiscal policy. On Wednesday the "Daily Mail," which has hitherto supported the Government, threw them over, and declared that the situation was made intolerable by the dissen- sions in the Unionist:camp. This is a pretty clear symptom of disorganisation. The supporters of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN cannot contain their impatience. Mr. BALvona. has forfeited their confidence. Still they might rally to the call of the Whips if the Leader of the Opposition were to move a vote of censure. However, a full debate on the extra- ordinary attitude Mr. BALFOUR has adopted would be advantageous, and it might be well for Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANNERMAN to bring it about without delay. The Government business is falling out of gear. Not a single Bill has yet been introduced. Mr. ARNOLD-FOSTBR'S continuous explorations show that the Army muddle is as great as ever. Ministers are doing nothing which excites even the smallest interest on their own side. We were told that Mr. BALFoun ought to stay in office until the Far Eastern War is over, as he alone is competent to maintain british interests in the difficult negotiations that will arise over the settlement of peace. We see no sign that Mr. BALFOUR is competent to maintain anything: A Minister who allows his own personal credit to suffer so much is not exactly the man to represent his country in:any international crisis. Mr. BALFOUR has initiated a procedure which would bring the House of Commons into utter disrespect if it were established as a preced3nt. He denies the right of the House to express an opinion except under conditions which he chooses to impose. No Prime Minister has ever before arrogated this prerogative —not even Prime Ministers like Palmerston, Disraeli, and Gladstone,wlao exercised an authority and weilded a power which Mr. BALFoun is never likely to attain.

The meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening will be more than ordinarily interesting. In addition to the consideration of the estimates of expenditure for the various committees and the fixing of, the rates during the ensuing year,there are several items on the agenda which should provoke animated discussion.

Public salaried appointments are invariably the subject of keen interest among the community immediately concerned, and there can be no surprise that the Deputy Town Clerkship of War- rington is eagerly discussed wherever men do con- gregate. The post is probably the most important of the subordinate Corporation offices and it is a considerable time since there was a vacancy such as is caused by Mr. Stanley Wilson's promotion to the Borough of Leigh. A': sub-committee of the General Purposes Committee has already considered and reported on the matter and made recommendations which will be discussed at Tuesday's meeting. The Deputy- Town Clerk's duties are defined under ten separate heads, and they are of a most onerous and respon- sible character, involving the appointment of a qualified solicitor. The sub-committee recommends ' a salary of £175 rising by annual increases of £l5 to a maximum of £250. Here will probably be found the bone of contention—at least we hope so for the credit of Warrington. One hundred and seventy five pounds for a fully-qualified solicitor expected to carry out the duties of Deputy Town clerk to a Borough of 70,000 inhabitants and possessing a rateable value of £250,000 seems to us paltry in tho extreme. There are men working under the Corporation who have had no profes- sional training, and who require to pass no examinations, who can command an equal sum. There is no premium on brains offered here, and we are convinced that th 3 Council will not be con- sidering the best interests of the town or even acting fairly to the members of the legal profession unless it offers a more substantial salary for the man who will be required to act as Mr. Lyon Whittle's deputy. There is ample evidence of a false notion of economy in the sub-committee's proposals and we hope they will be revised on a basis commensurate with the dignity of the borough and of the office which is rendered vacant by Mr. Wilson's removal.

Twelve months months ago, in commenting upon the financial pos•tion of the borough as indicated by the annual budget statement,we remarked : "It- will be no mean achievement if the year terminates Iwith a balance in the bank,instead of,as at present, due to the bankers ' " We are pleased to be anle to congratulate the Council and officials upon having accemolished the feat, for the deficit of £3,362 on the Borough Rate account has dwindled to £li, while in place of the bank overdraft of £2766 10i. on the Improvement Rate account, there is now a credit balance of £551 153. 63. Of course it must not be forgotten that a large pro.

portion of the increased call last year was necessi- tated by these two items, and that rates were levied to meet the deficits. What is more important to the lay mind, which is not much concerned about the fluctuations of bank balances, is the fact that the total of the rates is less by 2d. than it was a year ago. Instead of the increase which the overburdened ratepayers have tremblingly anticipated they will be called upon to pay 7s. Bd. in the pound instead of 7s. 10d. The good news is all the more welcome because of its unexpectedness. When it was announced a few weeks ago that the call for educational purposes was increased by £1,435 7s. 11d, or about lid. in the pound, there was no hope in the town that the final rates would be less than Bs., but the fact that the money owing to the bank had been met by last year's heavy call was over- looked. The reduction in the rates, ho sever, is not at all an indication that the town is costing less to govern and maintain. In fact the opposite is the case, the tendency still being for the rates to go up. We would not willingly damp the satis faction which will be felt by thn ratepayers at the ' reduction of al. which has been effected. A reduc- tion, however small, and for whatever reason. is hailed as a shower would be on the parched plains of India. Twopence is twopence—and consider- ably more when it is "in the pound." Had it been sixpence we might have hoped that there was a possibility, by careful management, of bringing the rates down to a normal level. But the fact has to be faced that with the increased cost of education and town improvements nothing but a tight hand will keep the upward tendency of the rates within moderate bounds.

The estimates of nearly all the,Committees show a slight increase, but nothing beyond what might reasonably be expected. Last year over £l,BOO was cut out of the estimates at the last moment; this year they have practically passed through unscathed. The only item which has been refused sanction is one of £2OO for the exten- sion of the Library scheme, the Museum Committee again having appealed fruitlessly for this modest sum. When it is reflected that the Council by special resolution two years ago agreed to a scheme for a provision of three delivery stations, and a reading room at Silver street, it seems the height of • inconsis- tency for them to persist in refusing the money. Two hundred pounds is a very small sum compared with the utilitarian advantages which must accrue from its investment in this direction. It is a matter for regret that the operation of the Education Act caused Councillor Upson's retirement from the Council. He stood sponsor to the scheme, and would, we think, have carried it through are this. Perhaps Alderman Bennett will once more take up the cudgels and endeavour to persuade his somewhat mercenary- minded confreres that an extension of the facilities for reading can only result in the benefit of the community.

The road-foremanship is to be finally considered at a special meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee on Monday, Old will be discussed by the Council through the med!ure of a suspension of the Standing Orders the following evening. As the advertisement indicated, the duties now in- clude those of the macadam road foreman, but it is somewhat of a surprise to find that none of the three men who were favourites a month ago are requested to appear before the committee on Monday. A new set of applications appears to have been received, though the additional duties are not of a particularly exacting or difficult nature. However there is probably no likelihood of a recurrence of the friction which arose from the committee's recommendation to the earlier appointment.

We are anxious to join nearer friends and aquaintances in our expressions of regret at the death of Alderman Sutton. He was warmly esteemed in the town which has witnessed the passing of nearly all his days, and which has been served faithfully by him during the years of a long life. There was unassuming amiability 'about the late Alderman's manner which won him friends even among foes—if we can so describe those who differed from him on political and other grounds. He was never bitter—a fact which was often evident when he crossed swords with an adversary in the Council Chamber. An unsparing sarcasm tinged with humour served him instead of sharper weapons. Alderman Sutton was a worthy War- ringtonian,and his departure from our midst leaves a distinct sense of public loss.

As was expected, Alderman Benn,tt had suc- ceeded to the chairmanship of the Education Com- mittee and there is a consensus of opinion that he well deserves.the honour. He has taken a keen interest in the workkg of the new act in Warring- ' itoownlnang so le end daltahbougahheexwpielrl experienced a irnia a difficult task akis m nfort F. Monks, we have no doubt that his conduct in the chair will lack nothing for want of earnest en- deavour. We would, however, counsel Alderman Bennett to do that which is foreign to his nature. It is essential that he should at all times be firm rather than conciliatory. At any rate he must be firm first and conciliatory afterwards, if the members of the committee are to be kept in order. Several times at Wednesday evening's meeting they showed a tendency to get out of hand, and ALierman Bennett, who has a great regard for the dignity of the authority, would do well in future to maintain a strict com- pliance with the rules governing the committee's procedure. It is a matter for congratulation that the committee has succeeded in retaining the services of .tilderman Pierpoint and we hope he will make special efforts to prove himself a capable and hard-working vice-chairman now that he has consented to accept office.

Education is receiving quite its due share of public discussion in Warrington, and various aspects of the question have been in the forefront during the past week. The members of the Teachers' Association held their annual meeting on Saturday and by a thoughtful address Mr. S. W. Jeffery, the newly elected president, at once proved his fitness for the office. With his advocacy of a national system of education we are in complete accord, for, as he well pointed out, education is not a parochial question, but a matter of great concern to 1, the nation at large. Therefore the charge for it should come upon the national Exchequer. There are two very distinct sides to the subject of smaller classes which Mr. Jeffery was equally emphatic in pressing. While a greater restriction of the number of scholars to be controlled by each teacher would doubtless increase the standard of efficiency, it would involve the employment of more teachers and a correspondingly augmented expenditure. Whethcr the ratepayers would be willing ta make the necessary sacrifice at present is a question open to doubt, and by setting the pace too rapidly in these matters there is a danger- of a revulsion of feeling to the detriment of educa- tion. Saturday also witnessed an interesting event at Penketh, and the members of the Society of Friends are to be congratulated upon the hand- some extension of the co-educational school which is doing such a useful work there.

England has not a littlo to learn from America in regard to education, and some valuable details of the system built up by our transatlantic cousins were presented by Mr. E. S. A. Robson, head- master of the Secondary School, in a lecture on Wednesday evening. The great advantage which the Americans enjoy is absolutely free education 'to the ago of eighteen, and children can pass through both elementary and secondary schools without having to pay fees or purchase books. In Warrington we are happily making progress in the provision of secondary education, but full advantage cannot be taken of the facilities by all to whom they would be of undoubted benefit owing to the somewhat prohibitive charges. The Education Committee has taken a step in the right direction in empowering head teachers of elementary schools to nominate a limited number of children for free scholarships to the higher institution.

Children's Dangerous Practice. The danger attending the practice, unfortunately common amongst children, of hanaing on" behind lurries and other yehicles,was illustrated by an accident which befel Reginald Wilkes, aged nine years, of 22, Atherton-street on Monday afternoon. He was riding on the end of a lurry in Factory-yard when he was pulled off by another boy. He fell to the ground, and the wheel caught his leg, inflicting a wound. He was taken to the Inarmary, and detained for treatment. ASK for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASb MINERAL

Action by Sir Gilbert Greenall. Sir Gilbert Greenall, of WarraTton, was the plaintiff and Mr. W. J. 'Patera, a Cardiff ship-owner, the defendant in a horse case which was tried on IThursday in the King's Bench Division, before the Lord Chief Justice and a special jury. Mr. Shea, K.C., said the plaintiff had bought ithe mare Mel Valley Princess from the defendant for ..f.,500. Then he found that she was a confirmed " wind-sucker," and therefore of no use to him for breeding. She had been resold as a ", wind-sucker " for ;£250, and the present claim was for the balance of £250, with .4.:28 ;is expenses. Mr. Tatem said he gave £7OO for the mare in 1002,and he had since exhibited her at shows with great success. When she was resold for ;c250 he bought her back, giving the parc baser a profit, and she was now in as good condition as ever she was. The jury gave a verdict for the defendant, and judgment followed.

Workingmen's Mission. The third Band of Hope entertainment of the season was held in the schoolroom on Tuesday evening, when Mrs. Simpson presided. The programme opened with a well-executed pianoforte solo by Miss B. Leigh. Recitations were given by Misses Kinsey, A. Taylor, L. Kendrick, G. Hughes, M. Gilbert, G. Morris, H. Cox ; Mastersl Dennett and C. Foster. Miss Cook and Mrs. Gray contributed solos, for which they received hearty applause. A. pianoforte and mando- line duet was given by Miss Leigh and Miss Thomp- son. The dialogue " Good and bad management, was given by Bessie Foster, Sarah Parkinson, Hilda Morris and Gertie Morris in a pleasing manner. Charlotte Jones and Bessie Foster sang a duet in good style. The dialogue " Cousin Alice's Story " was given by Clara foster, Charlotte "Jones and Emily Foster. Harry Rowley, Harry Pritchard, Arthar Morris' and Ernest Whitley recited "The Chain of Drink." The dialogue " Seaside Lodgings " was given by the members of Sankey Bridges Band of Hope, and was much enjoyed. A very successful and enjoyable entertainment concluded with the Doxology.

H. M. ASHTON'S LIFE TONIC is the KING OF TONICS.-63, SANKEY ST., WARRINGTON.—AdVt•

County Council's Experiments. Two interesting pamphlets have just been issued by the Agricultural Department of the Education Com- mittee of the County Council of Lancaster and contain information of considerable value to agriculturalists. The first booklet gives the report of an experiment on different varieties of potatoes, conducted at the County Council farm. It is pointed out that year by year agriculturists have to decide as to the most suitable variety of potato for their respective re- quirements. Very many varieties are on the marltet, some of which are good, some had, and some indiffer- ent, and some are not known at all by the goneral community. New varieties are often very costly, and before their true value can be known they must be tested in various ways, and if they fail to stand the test they soon pass out of cultivation. Seeing that new varieties are so expensive, and that an adequate comparison of several varieties involves a large amount of time and trouble, which the ordinary farmer would hardly care to give—although some do a great deal in this directioa—it was decided by the committee to test a few new varieties grown extensively at the present time. The results are presented in full. The second pamplet contains reports of experiments on the manuring of potatoes, and the planting of potatoes at different distances apart. The experi- ments in each case have been conducted by Mr. John Bayne, M.A., B. Sc., principal of agriculture, and Mr. Edward Porter, B. Sc., N.D.A., lecturer in agriculture. PUBLIC BENEFIT. Volunteer Sergeants' Annual Dinner. The 3rd annual sergeants' dinner, in connection with the Ist. V.B. P.W.V., South Lancashire Regiment was hold at the Winmarleigh Cafe, on Saturday even- ing, covers being set for about 60. The company included Colonel Edlesten, Majors. J. D. Fairclough, B. Fairclough, and F. M. Appleton, Captains Hutchinson, Potter, Williamson and Avery, Lieutenants J. F. Smith, and H. 0 wen, Mr. W. Owen, Mr. Greenall, and Sergeant-Major Rawlinson. The following representatives also attended from the Bth and 40th Depot —Sergeant-Major Delmage, Colonel- Sergeants Kelly and Nevvall, and Sergeant Roberts. After dinner, which was served in the Café's usual satisfactory manner, there was a very attractive pro- gramme, Colour-Sergeant Cliff (president of the Sergeants' Mess), being in the chair. "The , Health of the King" was given by the ! Chairman, and was enthusiastically received. I The next toast was "Colonel Edlesten and Officers of the Battalion," given by Quartermaster Sergeant Willcocks, and was responded to by Colonel Edlesten, in a pithy speech, full of good advice to all present. Colour-Sergeant Cliff then gave "The Army and Navy," which was responded to by Major F. M Appleton, in the absence of the Adjutant, Captain Bundock. "The Visitors," by Sergeant-Major Ingram, was responded to by Sergeant-Major Delmage. The rest of the evening was passed very enjoyably by all present, with songs, recitations, and brief speeches. The following was the programme :---Major B. Fairdough, song, "Sweet Marie"; Sergeant F. A. Coulden, song, "Good-bye, Little Yellow Bird " ; Mr. H. Sutton, song, "The Powder Monkey," also " Navaho " ; Mr. W. Owen. song, "The Veteran's Song" Mr. J. Broadhurst, song, " Anchored " ; Mr. Maynard, song, "The Serenaders," and a recitation, "The Lincoln " ; Sergeant Walker, song, "Old Bull and Bush" ; Captain Williamson, song, Oh, promise me"; Colour-Sergeant Taylor, song, "In Old Madrid " ; Colour-Sergeant Kelly, song, "Callaghan," and also another song. Mr. Sutton acted as accompanist.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 1, 1905. DEATH OF ALDERMAN SUTTON.

Warrington Ragged School Mission. The pretty cantata " Red Riding Hold" and miscellaneous concert recently given at the Parr Hall by the pupils of Miss Williams' school were repeated on Monday evening at the Ragged School. Nearly 100 performers took part in the entertainment, which was excellently rendered before a crowded audience. The good singing and clever acting of the children were evidence of the great labour and pains Miss Williams and her assistants must have bestowed in training them. The entire proceeds, £3, were handed over to the Mission funds by Miss Williams, and to that lady and her clever pupils,the Executive tendered their hearty thanks.

UNDER T-lIE CLOCK for Good BOOTS.—Advt.

Salmon in the Mersey at Warrington. On Saturday last two well-known local anglers, Samuel Thomas and William Boyle, were walking along the river bank by the six-acre reach between Walton Arches and Messrs. Kenyon's Works when their attention was directed to the efforts of some small boys who were endeavouring to catch a fish in a puddle of water left by the recent high tides. The two anglers succeeded in capturing the fish and after Encuring a bait-can conveyed it alive to the Town Hall where it was placed in the aquarium. The fish, how- ever, did not long survive the struggles which it had gone through and it was found dead on Monday morn- ing. It is a salmon smolt or young salmon just past the parr stage, and when captured was in splendid condition, its sides glistening like bars of polished silver. Several shoals of fish, in all probablity,young salmon or smolts, were seen in the river and watched by :an interested crowd on the bank. It is also reported that several porpoises were seen at Bank Quay. There can be little doubt that if the pollution of the river was stopped it would soon be stocked with salmon and other fish and the river would become famous as a salmon river as in the days of a hundred years ago.

Sankey, Stott, Wright, Fulton Smith, Benson, Barton, Brough, Skelland, Clegg, Hulse, Lrd, Wilson and Magian. The Borough Bench was represented by :—Messrs. John Fairclough, F. Monks, Henry Roberts, A. J. Jolley, J. W. Hayward, R. T. Fairclough, W. Roberts, O. Jackson Holmes, H. Thornton, Dr. Fox, J. Rymer Young, and G. F. F. White (magistrates' clerk). AMERICAN EDUCATION. Free Tuition to of Eighteen.

INTERESTING LECTURE BY MR. E. S. A. ROBSON.

Mr. E. S. A. Robson, M. Sc., headmaster of the War- rington Secondary School and Technical Institute, recently visited America with the object of collecting information concerning the elementary and higher education systems in operation there. Some of the most important results of his inquiries were interestingly sub- mitted in a lecture on " American Education," which Mr. Robson delivered in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- street, on Wednesday evening, under the auspices of the Warrington Polyglot Society. There was a goof attendance, Mr. T. May (president of the society) pre- siding. The members of the Education Committee present were :—Alderman Smethurst, Rev. John Yonge, Mr. W. Bibby, Mr. A. J. Jolley, Mr. Holman Kingdon, and Councillor J. Fulton Smith. The CIIAIRMAN remarked that as they were anxious to remedy the backward state of educa- tion in this country, he was sure that audience would be glad to know what the Americans were doing. There was no one in this country apparently who was able and willing to do what an American had recently done for education by giving ten millions to one institution. It made one wish that we had a few men like Mr. Rockefeller in this country. Mi. RonsoN then proceeded to outline the educational scheme in the United States and by means of lantern slides showed the generous provision made for the mental and physical training of the pupils. Attention was drawn to the fact that in America free education is provided up to the age of 18 years in the Elementary and Secondary Schools ; this system obviates any difficulties arising out of the poverty of the parents. The lecturer also noted the weakness of English educa- tion insomuch as the small number of scholarships pre- cluded a number of pupils from participating in the benefits of higher education. In his opinion the recent action of the Warrington Education Committee was a step in the right direction, as for the future power was given to the headmasters of the elementary schools to select a number of children for free places at the Secondary School. The Americans laid great stress on manual training and the large sums of money expended in the Manual Training High Schools and in the Technical Institutes resulted in greater efficiency of the artisans and engineers. Such institutions as the Masachusetts Institute of Technology and the Cornell University were far ahead of anything, existing in I Great Britain, both from the point of view of equip- ment and the calibre of the students. The Universities of America were of a very mixed character, some being of first class while others were just as inefficient. The lecturer noted the tendency of the American University to drop culture studies and go in for more utilitarian curricula.

The pleasure of the descriptive account of educa- tional institutions was enhanced by tfie pictures thrown on the screen, and the views of New York,Washington, and Niagara Falls were particularly beautiful. At the conclusion of the lecture a vote of thanks to Mr. Robson was proposed by Mr. OCKEL ant seconded by M. SECRETAN, whose remarks were given in French. The proceedings terminated with a vote of thanks to the lanternist (Mr. Kertland) for his able services, and to the Co-operative Society for the use of the lecture room.

RANDALL MEMORIAL CONCERT IN PARR HALL.

The concert promoted by a committee of which Mr W. H. Payton, Hug. Bac., Mr. T. D. Amos and Mr. A Clive were prominent members, with the object of benefitting the widow and family of the late Mr. Percy Randall, did not draw the numerous audience that might have been expected. Quite an array of local names of singers had been announced which, with the weight of Mr. T. J. Down's orchestra and Mr. R. H. Pearson's services as solo violinist thrown in, made up an impos- ing list of artistes. As a matter of fact it early became apparent that there was in reality a surfeit of both performers and programme items. What with unduly prolonged pieces and numerous recalls unfortunately construed into demands for more, the evening's enter- tainment was spun out to a wearisome length. Owing to some unfortuitous concurrence of circumstances it happened, too, that several of the songs were both unattractive and ineffective for concert-room purposes ; quite apart from the merit or the demerit of their rendering. Among these may be instanced Mr. Fred Fieldhouse's "When bright eyes glance " (Helgeeock), Miss M. W. Collin's " Daffodils a-blowing " (German) and Mrs. Arthur Booth's "Rebecca's prayer" (Ivanhoe), than which last mentioned it would be difficult to find an excerpt which could suffer more by the enforced severance from its proper surroundings of ; scenery costume and circumstance. In the later part of the programme Mrs. Booth had a second opportunity which she used to some- what better advantage, Miss M. V. White's song "The spring has come "being much more lyrical although it too, has little enough in it of potency to touch the listener's sympathy. The "Polacca ' (A. Goring Thomas), served its purpose very well in bringing to the front a new singer,Miss Pollie Houghton,and of display- ing the fine range of a soprano voice of promising sweetness and flexibility. Responding to acclamation with "The Swallows " (Cowen) one's good impressions were confirmed in regard to this young vocalist, whose development and career will, we opine, be watched with some interest by the Warrington musical public. A quintet comprising Miss G. Pemberton, Miss M. W. Collin, Mr. J. H. Draper, Mr. F. Fieldhouse and Mr. ,T, W. Bibby gave the item "Love is meant to make us glad" (from Merric England) with fine zest and in good style, and, later, Miss Pemberton sang the song "Sun- shine and Rain" (Blumenthal) in a most satisfactory way. The songs by Mr. J. W. Bibby and Mr. Rabbitt were the best feature of the concert. In "Lend me your aid" Mr. Rabliitt chewed himself in fine form, the opening recitative being declaimed with so much earnestness and feeling that the effective rendering of the moving appeal of the "Aria" was never felt to be a matter of doubt. Being greeted with quite enthusiastic applause Mr. Rabbit essayed "Take a pair of sparkling eyes," a song which he would seem to have lately made his own. Mr. Bibby's "Down in the deep" (Petrie), while admirably vocalised, was sung with too little visible feeling to be entirely successful. Yet it, too, won its encore—"in happy moments" (Wallace), in the interpretation of which Mr. Bibby was quite at home. The single number ("Nellie Gray ") set down in the programme for the male-voice quartet (Messrs. R. W. Cook, W. Critchley, P. George and W. Woodcock) was very tastefully and pleasingly sung by thersolo voice, and the ensemble would have been entirely satisfactory had the banjo accompaniment of the three other voices been made less assertive. In the encore item " Home Sweet Home " opportunity was found of engaging in more legitimate work, a very creditable rendering being here given of a piece of most interesting part-writing. Mr. Pearson' violin solo " Ifejre Kati" (tlubay) was nicely diversified in its two movements and being on the whole well played it was scarcely surprising that yet another encore piece—a romance of Berthold Tours's—had to be given to satisfy the clamourous audience. The pieces by the late Mr. T. J. Down's orchestra— now under the bat On of Mr. Arthur Down—bulked largely in the programme. Would that we could proportionately conrpliment the instrumentalists col- lectively and individually on the quality of their work. It is to be regretted that the various sections of the orchestra are handicapped from the beginning by faulty intonation ; the cornet soloist offending most seriously in this respect in the tediously long medley entitled " Morrie England." In matters of taste generally the orchestra would be infinitely benefitted by being held with a tighter rein. A meed of praise is duo to Mr. IV. H. Payton Mus. Bac. for his most tasteful accompaniments. We understand that a sum of about £26 will result from this concert enterprise.

The " Father " of the Council,

By the death of Alderman Thomas llindmarsh Sutton, ,J.P., Wa..rington loses one of its most honoured townsmen and the Town Council its oldest member. The end was not unexpected, but, coming as it did in the sense of a personal loss t) a very wide circle, it evoked the deepest regret. For some con- siderable time it had been evident that the deceased alderman's health was breaking up. The death of his wife about three years ago was a heavy blow, and the anxiety told so severely upon him that his friends felt much concerned. After the lapse of a few months he appeared to regain almost his wonted activity and once more entered whole-heartedly into the discharge of the public duties with which he had been so long identified. Illness, however, again overtook him and during the past six months ho was compelled to lay aside most work. He suffered acutely from sciatica, and despite the attentions of Dr. Adams and, in c saltation, Dr. Steel, of Manchester, he gradually became weaker and passed away shortly before noon on Tuesdcy in his seventy-first year. An honourable and strenuous career is thus brought to a close. Alderman Sutton was one of those War- ringtonians who by dint of their own abilities and per- severance have gained a foremost place in their native town. He was born in 1834 ia a house in the Market- place, now forming part of the premises of Messrs. Robert Davies and Co.,solicitors. Since those days the neighbourhood has undergone a marked change,for when Alderman Sutton lived there pleasant gardens brightened the surroundings, and there was no market hall. The latter part of the late Alderman's education was received at the Warrington Blue Coat School where 200 day scholars, in addition to the children on the foundation, had the benefit of a valuable commercial education At theage of fourteen he entered the office of the late Mr. Beamont, who was then the first Mayor of Warrington, and made gratifying progress. Mr. Bearnont took a particular interest in him and often gave him private lessons after ofifice hours. After remaining eight years ' in Mr. Bennett's service, Mr. Sutton accepted the position of cashier and managing clerk in the office of Mr. George Webster, a well known Liverpool solicitor, and acted in that capacity for eight or nine years. The next step in his career was to enter into the business of auctioneer and valuer with his brother, Mr. J. C. Sutton, who subsequently relinquished the workand devoted himself entirely to Poor Law matters, obtaining the appointment of Clerk to the Board of Guardians which he at present holds. The business has since remained in the hands of Alderman Sutton and it was a tribute to the confidence reposed in him that he was often engaged to value property in connection with arbitration proceedings necessitated by street improve- ments. In 1859 Alderman Sutton was married to Miss Rogers, the daughter of a well-known professional gentleman in Worcester, and she too was held in high esteem by the townspeople. As a staunch Conservative, Alderman Sutton was prominently identified with the party organization in Warrington and in the neighbouring constituency of Newton. About thirty-five years ago he was appointed registration agent and secretary by the Conservatives in the Borough, his knowledge of registration law particularly fitting him for the position. At the same time he had thelconduct of the revisions and elections in of about twenty different townships in South-West Lan- cashire. He was one of the Executive Committee at Liverpool who selected Mr. Richard Assheton Cross (now Lord Cross) to contest South-West Lancashire in 1868 against Mr. Gladstone. In Warrington Parliamen- tary Elections he was always to the fore, and more especially when the late Sir Gilbert Greenall sought the suffrages of the electors. Up to the time of his death he retained the oifice of hon. secretary to the IConservative Association, and was also hon. secretary of the Warrington polling district of the Newton Parlia- mentary division. For upwards of thirty years Alderman Sutton served continuously as a member of the Town Council and dis- played the keenest interest in all matters touching the progress of the municipality. In 1874 he successfully contested the South West Ward (now St. Austin's Ward) in the Conservative interest, and rapidly proved his capabilities for Town Council work. His valuable services were recognized in 1886 when he was elected to the Aldermanic bench, whilst in the following year the highest honour of the Council was con- ferred upon him by his unanimous election as Mayor of the Borough in succession to the late Alderman Pickmere. Lie discharged the duties ably and impartially for two years. During the whole of his public career Alderman Sutton was most assiduous in his attention to committee work and took particular interest in the Health and Street Improvement Com- mittees of which he was chairman. At the time of his death he was also a member of the Watch, Town Hall, Parks and Baths, Rivers, Electricity and Tramways, and General Purposes Committee. In addition he was a representative member of the Education Committee, represented Warrington on the Manchester Port Sanitary Authority, and served, on behalf of the Council, on the governing body of the Warrington Grammar School. At the Town Council meetings the late Alderman entered Into the various debates with considerable ardour. He was always outspoken and never hesitated to express what to him was a conviction, no matter how numerous or powerful were his opponents. He was generally regarded as the wag of the Council, and his remarks were often illumined with flashes of wit which created a ripple of merriment in the Council Chamber. As a justice of the peace Alderman Sutton was very regular lin his attendance at the Police Court, having been a member of the Bench since 1886 when he was appointed by the crown. A beneficent work which the late alderman had very closely at heart was the provision of an annual treat to the aged poor of the town. For a long period he held the office of president of the committee which under- took the arrangements, and his cheery presence at the gatherings was a source of delight to the old folk by whom he will be greatly missed. Although he was far from well, he attended the last treat held in the Parr Hall early in the new year and delivered an interesting address.

Of the many other organizations with which Alderman Sutton was identified it is impossible to speak fully. At one time he was a member of the Board of Guardians and discharged the duties with ability. He was actively connected with St. Paul's Church and had held the office; of sidesman and warden, taking the deepest interest in the progress of the church and its various organizations. He was the last hon. secretary of the Mechanics' Institute, while the Blue Coat Brotherly Society had in him a valuable vice-president. Another useful institution which claimed his support was the Savings' Bank and he performed good service as a manager. Few gentlemen commanded more general respect than the late Alderman Sutton,his geniality not less than his devotion to public work making him one of the most popular figures in his native town. He leaves five sons with whom much sympathy is expressed in their breave- ment.

THE FUNERAL,

There was a striking demonstration of respect and sympathy at the funeral which took place at the Ceme- tery yesterday. The large representation of public bodies was evidence of the active part which the deceased alderman took in the town's affairs and of the esteem in which he ,has been held by all sections of the corn- tnunity. The cortege left the deceased's late residence in Springfield-street at 11.30 and was joined by a large number of coaches conveying members of the Corpora- tion. Education Committee and other public bodies. At the head walked a posse of police in charge of the Chief Constable (Mr. Luke Talbot) and Chief Inspector Phillips. On the route many townspeople ;had gathered to pay a last tribute of respect. The service in the Cemetery Chapel was con- ducted by the Rev. W. Bracecamp, vicar o St. Paul's, and at the graveside the Rev. H. V. Pigot, vicar of Stockton Heath and graveside, of St. Paul's, officiated. The last rites were witnessed by a large assembly. Magisterial Reference.

Reference was made to the late Alderman Sutton before the commencement of the ordinary business at the Warrington police courts on Wednesday. Alderman Hutchinson who presided on the Bench said that on behalf of the magistrates be desired to express his sincere regret at the death of their dear old friend and brother magistrate, Alderman Sutton. His death would be a great loss to the bench and to everyone who knew him. He was a gentleman probably more capable than most people as he was :keen and far-seeing in matters connected with justice. He was a very old magistrate and they all deeply regretted his death. They had great sympathy for hie family and friends. The Chief Constable, on behalf of the police force- desired to humbly and respectfully associate himself with the expression of sympathy and regret. During the eleven years he had been in Warrington, Alderman Sutton had been a magistrate and a member of the Watch Committee. Alderman Sutton was the father of the Council, and also the father of the Bench, and he was sure no one would miss him from the Bench and Watch Committee more than the members of the police force, who would miss both his wise head and kindly heart.

REFERENCE AT THE EDUCATION COMMITTEE MEETING.

At the annual meeting of the Education Committee held on Wednesday evening, Alderman Bennett, the newly-elected chairman, said : It is with great regret that have to annonnce the death of Alderman Thomas Sutton, the father of the Council, a member of our committee, and a representative governor of the Grammar School. In consequence of his many duties and his increasing infirmity, Alderman Sutton was not able to take that active part in the work of this cora; mittee which he would have liked, but be was always deeply interested in its proceedings. This is not the time or place to speak of all his other multifarious services to the town of his birth. Every- body knew him and everybody liked him, and it will be long indeed before his genial place is filled. lam sure I shall be expressing your united wishes when I move : That this committee desires to place'on record the great loss the town has sustained by the death of Alderman Thomas H. Sutton, J.P., and to convey to his family their deep symptithy with them in their bereavement. Alderman Pierpoint said he had a melancholy satis- faction inseconding. He was sure it needed no words of his to impress the fitness of the resolution. The resolution was carried in silence.

STOCKTON HEATH MATTERS. Should Urban Powers be Sought.

RECREATION GROUND REQUIRED.

The ratepayers of Stockton Heath discussed a variety of subjects at their annual meeting which was held in the National Schools on Thursday evening. One of the principal items under consideration was the future , position of Stockton Heath in respect of the manage- ment of parish affairs. I Mr. Clarke presided over a small attendance and in a brief speech reviewed the year's work in the parish. One question which had arisen was that of the supply of electricity in the district. That question he said , had been thoroughly thrashed out and had died so quickly that he thought there was no intention on the part of the Corporation to take electricity into the dis- trict for some time to come. Mr. Clarke proceeded to refer to a question which has agitated the minds of Stockton Heath residents for some time—the question of the tram fare to be charged by the Corporation for the journey from Rylands'-street, to Victoria-square. The decision of the Corporation to charge 11c1. was he thought unwise. The whole thing was a business transaction and in order to make the line pay it would have to be popular, and it would not be popular if a 11,d. fare was charged. He thought the extra charge of a half-penny was Out on as a sort of punishment to Stockton Heath residents for their not having fallen in with all the ideas of the Corporation. There was one advantage, however,theylwore not bound to pay lAd. He did not think there would be any diffi- culty with regard to workmen's cars, as when the time I came he thought the matter would only have to be mentioned and cheap cars would be run. Another ques- tion to which Mr. Clarke referred was that of the increase in the rates. Their expenditure was constantly going up, and the ratepayers wanted to know how it was they were getting no benefit from the increased lexisenditure. The expenditure upon education in the county was getting beyond all bounds and was altogether out of reason. The expenditure upon what was termed higher education was being rushed, and moreover he did not believe that the money was being used to the best advantage. So much money was going in clerks and officials' salaries, that education did not come in at all. Increased expenditure was also incurred by lighting and I improved sanitary arrangements. He could assure them I that there was no extravagance in those two directions. I (Hear, hear.) In answer to Mr. Deakin, Mr. Clarke stated that esa- mates were being prepared for the paving of Cawdor- street, and that the work would be proceeded with as foon as possible. Mr. J. W. Hall referred to the extinguishing of the lamps in the district. He advocated that the lamps in the main streets should be left lit all night, for the convenience of men woo had to go to work early in the morning. A promise was given that the Parochial Committee would consider the question and the matter dropped. Mr. England said he was pleased with Mr. Clarke's reference to the rates. He nevertheless felt that Mr. Clarke had not gone to the kernel when he mentioned the causes of the increase. At the last Parish Council, election the usual statements were made by candidates that they would keep the rates down. I Several members of the Parish Council at once pro- tested that they made no such statementHe was not,he said,making an invidious question ofit.lt was the usual thing at election times. Instead of the rates going down they were going up, and it appeared to him that the district was not being treated in an equitable way by the Runcorn District Council. To show that he would refer to what they were paying for roads. In Appleton there was four times the length of road that there was in Stockton Heath, whereas Stockton Heath contributed several times as much as Appleton for the maintenance of the roads. Another inequitable arrangement was the contribution under the Agricultural Relief Act. In consequence of the way in which the rates were levied Stockton Heath residents contributed about double the imount paid by people living in other parts of the district under the authority of the Rancorn District Council. Several years ago he said they were a set of noodles and he was prepared to admit again that they were noodles for allowing such arrangements to con- tinue. On the Parish Council they had some really in- telligent men—(hear, hear)—but apparently so great were the exertions of those men to get on the Council that they bad since been resting from their labours. The question of the rates was a serious one because it seemed to him that they were being legally robbed. During the last ten years the rates had actually doubled and as the money was not being spent upon Stockton Heath it was evident it was being spent in other I parts of the Union. One question which ought to be I immediately considered was the question of providing a recreation ground. He felt sure that before long they would rue the day when they failed to secure some land for that purpose. Mr. Hallows : People would look at the rates. Mr. England : It seems to me some members' of , the Parish Council are inclined to be par- simonious, and it is this parsimonious spirit which has kept the question of leaving a few lamps burning all night in the background. Continu- ing, he said he was sorry the Parish Council had not gone into the question of providing schools. The children of Stockton Heath were not being so well educated as they had a right to be. It was not the fault of the schoolmaster but they were not being taught many subjects which were being taught in the schools in Warrington. Mr. England proceeded to refer to the question of the tramways. He agreed with Mr. Clarke that it was purely a business arrangement and he felt sure it would right itself in time and that the Corpora- tion would before long reduce the fare to a penny.(Hear. kear.)The time was coming when they would seriously have to consider the question of forming themselves into an urban power or of joining Warrington. If they joined Warrington they would get excellent tell-us—perhaps fourteen years at the present rate and would secure all the benefits of the Corporation administration. On the other hand if they became an urban authority the rates would go up and would soon be Os. in the Z. Mr. Bouchier sympathised with much Mr. England had said, though he hardly agreed that they were not getting value for their money, though at the same time he thought that to a certain extent the rates had gone up in excess of the advantages they possessed. The question of obtaining urban powers was gone into years ago and they were in the same difficulty now as they were in at that time. If they obtained urban powers they would still have to contribute to the Runcorn Union poor rate and to the county and district rates. No one knew better than they did that Stockton Heath was the goose which laid the golden egg for the Runcorn Union. If they got urban powers the general rates would still be under the centrol of the Runcorn authorities. It would be necessary to levy a special rate of sd. or 6d. in the Z and the only advantage they would possess would be that they would have a greater power in deal- ing with their own affairs. With regard to education Mr. England spoke in one breath against expenditure and in the next breath said they were far too parsi- monious. There was no doubt that the school accommodation was not so good as it might be but he certainly did not agree with Mr. England that the education was not so good as it was in Warrington. He believed that the education was equal to the elementary education which was given in any school in Warrington. (Hear, hear.) New schools would cost from £lO,OOO to £12,000, two-thirds of which would have to be paid by Stockton Heath, and if a new school were built their rates would be a great deal heavier. What they wanted was a new school for Stockton Heath, Latchford, and Walton, and he thought it would be a good thing if the three districts would come to an arrangement and combine. He agreed with Mr. England with regard to the provision of a recreation ground, and I he was sure no one would grudge the expense thereby I incurred.

Mr. Rose: I would like Mr. England to state a specific instance where he has found elementary educa- tion in Stockton Heath was not so good as that in Warrington? Mr. England: In Peoples College three or four more subjects are taught than are taken in these schools. Mr. Clarke: Will you specify them? Mr. England: For the moment I cannot think what they are butl know that it is so. Mr. H. J. Westbiook replied at considerable length to Mr. England. It was not fair to compare the length of roads in Stockton Heath with the length of roads in another parish. They should compare the quality of the roads and the amount of traffic along them. With regard to agricultural land the farmer was entitled to some abatement from the rates because he did not need sanitary arrangements, lighting &c. He wanted it to be clear that they did not object to having new schools. If the ratepayers having had the cost explained to them would sanction the expenditure and not grumble afterwards, he would be perfectly willing to assist them in their desire. With regard to the tram fares Mr. Westbrook pointed out that when the line was complete to Victoria-square the route would be shorter than the Latchford route. That he thought gave them good grounds for asking that the fare should not be more than a penny. Seeing that the Corporation paid no rent for the use of the roads in the village he thought they had a right to ask that the fare should be less,rather than more than the fare charged on the Latchford route. Mr. Hallows said he hoped someone would move a resolution condemning the expenditure of the County Council. In the District Council the expenditure was closely watched, but in the County Council the members lavished money on their pets. Mr. Bradshaw pointed out that the District Council calls had also increased. He thought there was a little extravagance in the District Council. Mr. Hallows: That is due to the isolation hospital in the building of which the Council had no option. Mr. H. J. Westbrook moved "that this meeting of Stockton Heath ratepayers regrets the serious increase in the rates and asks the County Council and Runcorn District Council to exercise greater economy consistent with efficiency." Mr. Hallows seconded and the resolution was - , Mr. Bouchier asked if there was any diffef between the examinations in the school at St,

of instruction in Stockton Heath was the same as in the Leeds schools.

Mr. C. Clarke said the question of obtaining urban powers had been discussed by the Parish Council on many occasions. It was no use having urban powers and having to pay higher rates. Tha rates were not twice as high as they were ten years ago. During that period they had risen from 2s. 10d. to 4s. Gd. In advising them to join Warrington Mr. England , was trying to land them in double the rates they were now paying. He thought they should think twice before they followed a gentleman who had "House to let " in his window. (Laughter.) Mr. England : Although lam leaving the district I shall still be a ratepayer. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

PHYLLIS THE ELUSIVE.

[fly Godfrey Dean.]

Of Precedents and Phyllis.

Phyllis put down her book. " I had nearly givert you up," she said. Which was absurd. Of course, I knew I was late—quite five minutes— though I did look at the clock. I apz,logised. "Precedents," I said, very innocently, " are dangerous things." Once we all worshipped images, now it is precedents—and the lawyers are the great High Priests of this particular Temple. " What is the good of a precedent if it is not to be kept ?" she asked. "The existence of some precedents is only justified by the opportunities they offer one to break them.' Phyllis looked very intently at me and—yes— seemed hurt.

It would have been indiscreet, of .course, to notice it. A woman's feelings require the most careful treatment imaginable they are as delicate china compared with the earthenware of man's. " Phyllis "—my tone brought a quick glance f rota her--" when I consider how many weaknesses I have, I become depressed." "What of them?" she asked.

"They constantly remind me of my unworthiness of you. And you must see them, don't you ?" I could see her face well enough, but the long lashes of the eyes were lowered, masking the expres- sion that would tell me so much.

She made as if to speak, but no answer cams at once. Then, slowly, reluctantly, she said, '• of course as you mention it yourself I—l do see your weak- nesses."

I chilled at the words and the possibilities they contained for me. It was true; I have a great many weaknessess. Was she beginning to see them ? If so, it was better to face the inevitable (? early). No, the lover is not always on Olympian heights. For sixty seconds my imagination roved uncon- trolled. My mind jumped from point to point in dizzy fashion. Then I steadied myself with an effort. " Some people would think it too Much to ask that one should bear with so many as mine." " You are not an Admiral C"hrichton," she said. I winced. A man does not like to be even remind- ed that he is not perfection. "It is in y-iur hands; I prefer to face unpleasant truths." "You are speaking for yourself?" she asked. "Oh, entirely." "Still, I also think it best to face the truth." I turned my head to avoid seeing in her face any- thing strange to my eyes. " When it is unpleasant," she added. "Marriage may be a frightful tragedy," I said. "Yes," she replied; then paused. "Even with an Admirable Crichton."

Then how must she view the possibilities of marriage with one who, while negative of distinctive qualities, is positive in his weaknesses! "At least, I said, "you will never forget me, nor what you were—and ever will be—to ma?" She did not answer. It was natural she should be disturbed, but Time's hand smooths life's sharp edges. She looked up, and my eyes caught hers. There was, at least, pity in her face. "No," she said, " I will not forget." Her expres- pression was now one of curiosity. I stood up. It only remained for her to assist me and the difficulty would be over—if she wished it. Time, surely, is measured by the state of the mind. At last, " What is it to be, Phyllis ? " It was pleasant to use her name once more, and I lingered , over it. "You don't understand women one bit !" she burst forth, with flashing eyes. "How?" was all I could say, amazed at the change in her.

"Not even the elementary truth about *cm. Some men in searching for carious reasons for a woman's thoughts and actions are blind to the obvious." " What is it ?" I begged ?" " Do you suppose," she said, scorn in her voice at the idea," that a woman ever "—she faltered and turned her face away "Do you think," she resumed, quietly, " that women love men who have no weaknesses ? " This was illuminating, indeed. " No," Phyllis said, with a passionate emphasis that told how deeply her feelings were moved. " She does not love in spite of men's weaknesses, but more often because of them."

I am beginning to realise that the more one thinks one understands women the less one really does. "Won't you forgive me? If you only knew my feelings when I realised I was late " Words seemed banal. ‘! It is not that," she , said. " No! "—surprised. " That wouldn't have mattered a bit. but yon said —" " What ? " " You said something I did not like—about precedents." I tried to recall what I had said." "Oh, I remember, you said precedents were dangerous." Then revelation came, and with cruel force to me. It was ambiguous, though. "I did not mean it in that sense. How could you think it of me? " "Didn't you? But you said it immediately after I remarked that you were late." "That was unfortunate," I said. "And it seemed," Phyllis continued, 'as if you regretted--" I would not permit her to say more. I have regrets but not in that way. They are inspired by the know- ledge of man's very inferior nature. "This," I said, remembering a small circumstance, "is the particular edition I promised to get for you." "Was it this that made you late" she asked. "It was—in the fact, I forgot the cause." The words reminded Phyllis of her recent eloquent defence of women, and consciousness of it brought becoming blushes. " Your imagination," she said, " makes you think very foolish things." . . " Everything has its compensations," I said, later, "even foolish thoughts.,' " On reflection, and with the case in point to trate the fact, I see there are some precedents made sacred by inidvidual choice. And, as a lover; I recant of my heresy.

(To be continurAl.) (Those articles originally appeared in the "London Echo.")

Fire at the Lion Hotel.

Had it not been for the promptitude of P.C.'s Edwards and Tally and the members of the Fire Brigade, a fire which broke out in a coke, firewood and lumber-room under the ball room at the Lion Hotel early yesterday morning would probably have had serious consequences. On discoverma the outbreak one of the officers immediately roused the porter and servants at the hotel, whilst the other went to the Fire Station and gave the alarm. The brigade tarried out at full strength under Superintendent and Deputy-Superin- tendent Turnei and after about an hour's work extinguished the flames before any great damage was done. An ice-chest removed from the premises by the firemen bore evidences of the great heat, and the woodwork of the floor and doors of the room were charred.

A singular affair under the Education Act is reported from Marston, St. Limrence, near Banbury. In conse- nce of the rector persisting in taking the school ddren to church for religious instruction, in defiance af the regulations of the Northamptonshire County Council, that body have closed the school, and removed the children to the school in is neighbourino•' They have also withdrawn all financial support from the Marston school.

EARLESTOWN CRICKET CLUB.

Annual Meeting.

Tho annual meeting of the Earlestown Cricket Club was held in the Viaduct Institute,Earlestown on Friday night last, Mr. T. Ormand presiding over a fair gathering of members.

The minutes of the last meeting were read and approveel,after which the secretary(Alr. J. H. Alderson) read his annual report as follows :

SECRETARY'S REPORT.

During the past season nineteen matches were arranged for the Ist team, and thanks to a beautiful summer, these were all played. Eight were won, as compared with 7 the previous year, and 3 only in 1902, the improvement in this respect bsing well maintained seven were lost, as against 6 in 1903, and 6 in 1902, and 4 were drawn. Last season was a good scoring one,and runs were more numerous, 2630 fpr 162 wickets giving an average of 16-23, the average obtained in 1903 was 14.38 for 152 wickets. Here, too our opponents have shown .1 marked advance scoring 2621 runs for 171 wickets as against 1913 for 163, the average jumping from 11.12 to 15.32. T. Marsh is the leader in the batt- ing averages 26.41 for 15 innings, in 1903 he played 1 innings more and registered 18.12 which shows great improvement as the result of good steady cricket, 3. Crawley has not been with us long without making his presence felt, and has creditable figures, which give him the honour of scoring the biggest aggregate. I cannot pass on without mention- ing H. Barr whose consistent batting has been a strik- ing feature. In fact, it may be said he is the most improved player in the team, not only has he taken a high position in the batting figures, hut has also secured the prize given by Mr. Ormand for the best all round fielder. No special mention need be made of the bowling which has not been quite up to the standard of former years. Howarth and Alderson bore the brunt of the work with the ball. each securing 50 wickets, the former's average being 12.96 and the latter's 14.74. A great difficulty had been experienced in finding a suitable wicket keeper. G. B. Leigh entered the breach, with every credit to himself and all concerned, giving promise of becoming an excellent man in this trying position. The second eleven played 22 matches, 8 more than last year, win- ning 13 as compared with 9, losing 7as against 3, and drawing the same number (2) as last year. R. A. Robin- son easily comes out top. of the batting with the fine average of 34.42 for 15 •iningst scoring the highest aggregate, and the most in an innings, a remarkable score of 138. W. Harrison and H. Warburton deserve spelial mention for their excellenthowling records. The balance sheet which I have to present to you this evening shows the amount of cash received from all sources to be .Q 63 7s. 4d., including a balance brought forward at the commencement of the season of £1 ss. 3d.; subscriptions, £3O 4s. 63.; and gate receipts, £25 10s. 11d, being the chief items. The expenditure amounting to £62 19s. includes players, umpires and scorer's expenses, £2O 4s. 3d. ; professional assistance and talent money, £l7 ss. ; printing, Fcc., £7 19s. ; and cricket material, £9 2s. 3d.. leaving a balance in hand of Bs. 4cl; I might also add that the match with Leigh on August 20th was set aside for the benefit of George Husband, and the amount realised was £l3 18s. sd.

Most of you are aware that a new League has been formed under the title of the West Lancashire Cricket League. We have decided to join that body, and whether for good or evil, we are now committed for a season at least, to League cricket. When the sugges- tion to form a combination of local clubs was first made, we looked upon it very favourably indeed, believ- ing that is would make the game more popular in the district. But when it became apparent that a number of the strongest clubs that had been approached would not entertain the idea, our enthusiasm became some- what damped, seeing that we had to leave out of our list several clubs with whom we were very sorry to part. However, as I have already sttted, we were prepared to give it ft:trial, in the hope that after a season's working, the League may be strengthened by the inclusion of several clubs „which have up to now thought it advisable to hold aloof. Last year we entered our seJond team in the General Manager's Cup Competition, but it was unfortunate that two of the strongest matches had to be played at the commencement of the season whilst football was still running. We fully believe that it was entirely due to this that we did not figure in the tlral at London. The competition has this year been divided into two divisions, vrid we shall now be enabled to play our first team. We have been dragn to play at Earlestown lon June 10th against Crewe Permanent Way. Should we be successful in this match, we shall have the pleasure of meeting the winner of the Southern section at London, in the first week in August. It will thus be seen that the coming season will be full of interest to as, it only remains for the players to put forth their individual efforts to enable us to crown the present season with success.

• Arising out of the report Mr. John Owen said he had heard a lot of criticism with regard to the wisdom of joining a league. The Committee supported the idea of the formation of a league believing that it would give them the advantage of meeting the best clubs. It would be an expensive project for them, as in all pro- ability their expenses would total ZS or £9 per week and it behoved them to do their best for the club to try and make it a ..access. In this respect they would probably have to do something to put their finances on a firmer footing. They had already thought of approach- ing the Institute Committc 3 with a view to increasing the" gate." 2d. cc..s a ridiculously low charge for admission to a cricket match.

Mr. Ormcmd said the report was one of the most interesting he had listened to for several years. They had joined the league hoping it was for the good of the Earlestown Cricket Club for cricket in general.

After the chairman had compared the balance sheet with last year's sheet, both report and accounts were adopted. The election of officers was afterwards pro- ceeded with and resulted as follows :—President :—Mr. H. D. Earl, vice presidents :—Messrs, Smalley and Harwood, hon. secretary :—Mr. .1, H. Alderson, The Chairman here proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the Secretary for his report and for the able manner in which he had carried out his duties during the past year. This was approved. Mr. Ormand was re-elected hon. treasurer, Mr. C. W. Emmett was appointed Ist team captain, and Mr. J. H. Alderson vice-captain, whilst after Messrs. T. A. Li mood, and F. Reade had declined the doubtful honour. Mr. J. Burrell was appointed 2nd team captain, the election of an understudy being left over owing to the lack of volunteers. The following are nominated for the committee of nine, together with the number of votes received by each, viz :—Messrs. John Owen, 28 ; T. F. Roberts,2s ; T. Marsh, 22; J. Smith, 23; J. Sanders, 19 ; F. Reade, 22; G. Wilson, 19 ; T. A. Limond, 19 ; R. A. Robinson, N ; J. Seddon, 17 : G. B. Leigh, 14 ; W. Harrison, 15 ; J. S. Thane, 9 ; S. Henderson, 6 ; the first nine gentlemen therefore being elected. Messrs. J. Gooding and H. Tarbuck were re-appointed auditors. The prizes were afterwards presented, each recipient making a new departure by displaying his oratorical powers. Mr. J. Smith raised the question of increasing the annual subscription,andMr.E. J. W. Mull in's moved that the subscription be raised from 4s. to ss. The Chairman said they were out of order in discuss- ing the matter there and Mr. Mullins withdrew his proposition. Mr. Waddington and Mr. Orinand thought that a printed balance sheet should be circulated amongst the members a few days prior to the annual meeting. The usual votes of thanks brought the meeting to a close.

The Examiner, Saturday, April 1, 1905 STOCKTON HEATH PARISH COUNCIL. Further Increase in the Rates.

The question of the rates in the district was the principal question which agitated the minds of the members of the Stockton Heath Parish Council at their annual meeting which was held on Thursday evening. At the last meeting of the Runcorn Rural authorities, it was intimated that the district rate would be advanced in total to the extent of 3d in the S. This increase called forth a protest from the Stockton Heath representatives on the District Council, and a further test was forthcoming when the Clerk to the Parish Council announced that it was necessary to increase the special rates for the district by Is, lid. in the S. Mr, Clerk presided and at the meeting there were also present Messrs. J. Fairhurst, F. Warburton, F. Pearson, J. Whitley, H. J. Westbrook,J. Hallows, J. W. Bouchier, NV. Mclntyre, T. R. Westbrook, S. F. Welsby and E. Bradshaw (clerk).
