" Oh, rob away l" retorted Claretce Hedway, lightly. " I make a little money in my paper- staining business, and the loss of fifty pounds would not mean bankruptcy to me. Come, I will tempt you by betting that what I predict will happen before I start fur America again next week."

" Done !" said the author quickly. " Remem- ber you have only yourself to blame for this." " Or to congratulate !"

,• And so the be was made in due form. The rest of the voyage passed pleasantly for the two men in constant exchanges of knowledge and confidences. It must be admitted, however, that Tapton Futterby parted with more of both than did his companion. The author's nature was the more communicative.

" Your enemies are waiting for you to land, I see," remarked Clarence liedway to the afathor as the vessel drew alongside the dock wall at Southampton. _ _

" What do you mean ?" asked Tapton Flut- terby. " Why, the interviewers are mustered, cc I'm a Dutchman," Hedway replied. " That knot of men on the quay-side bears the Fleet Street

stamp, or I knOw nothing like so much of London as I pride myself I do." " I believe you are right," said the author, turning pale with vexation. How on earth am I to avoid them? I dodged the New York inter- viewers by locking mysAf in my state-room hours before the ship started, but I can't stay on board hours after she lands. Confound the her. viewen !"

" Right ! Let us confound them. Where do you put up when you get to town P" " At the Hotel Cecil."

" And you mean to reserve a compartment to yourself in the train from Southampton, I think you said F" " Yes." " Very well, then, the thing is quite simple. We will change names for an hour or two. You, the rich but unknown Mr. Hedway, land first with your luggage, see it past the Customs peo- ple and go on into your reserved compartment. Then I, the great 'lepton Flutterby, follow with my luggage, and of course gather all the inter- viewers about me. Some of them will no doubt accompany me to town in the unreserved com- partment in which I shall travel. That will not matter in the least. I have no rooted objection to the presence of interviewers, and they will be welcome to all they can get out of me. On reach- ing Waterloo, you drive with the luggage to my hotel, the Metropole, and then take yourself on to the Cecil, while I drive your luggage to the Cecil, and then betake myself to the Metropole. We thereupon resume our own names, and all ends happily, with the interviewers neatly dished. I shall quite enjoy carrying out the little plot. How does it strike you F' "Excellent, and very generous on your part." " Not at all generous. Or if you insist that it is, when it isn't, remember that I am your debt- or still for your admirable lessons in modern literature. Aliens, then. Here we are alongside. You lead the way, Mr. Hedway." The plot worked admirably, favoured by the close general resemblance of the two men. As Clarence Hedway had expected, the waiting journalists attached themselves to him, and some of them travelled with him in the same compart- ment to London, fully under the impression that they had Tapton Flutterby at bay. Their wiles, however, were of but little avail. The great author appeared deaf to their entreaties, literally as well as metaphorically. All they could get out of him, after superhuman shouting, was the repeated assurance that they would see all they wanted to know soon in the New York Daily Boom. Beyond imparting this precious piece of information, which of course only exasperated hie tormentors, the great man would be neither cajoled ner threatened. Well, Mr. Flutterby," said the real Simon Pure, as he greeted his late fellow-voyager on the latter's arrival with the luggage at the Hotel Metropole ; " what is it like to be an author pestered by interviewers, eh F." " Oh, jolly ! And how do you find the privacy of Mr. Hedway, the paper-stainer, when he travels P"

"Jolly, indeed! Well, thank you very much for your services. Come and dine with me to- morrow at the Cecil. I shall expect you without fail." " Thanks, I will come." Clarence Hedway kept his word, but hie wel- come at the Hotel Cecil the next evening was not of the warmest. His host had two newspapers in his hand, and looked mystified and resentful. "AIX, Mr. Hedway," he said, when his guest was announced, " I run puzzled and annoyed. Can you throw any light on these newspaper stories Y Read this one first."

Clarence Hedway took the London morning paper that was thrust into his hand, and read the marked report. It told of the landing of Mr. Tapton Flutterby, the distinguished author, at Southampton on the previous they, and of how he had journeyed to London in an ordinary carriage, and not in a reserved compartment, RH had. been expected. " The great novelist was as taciturn as usual in his bearing towards the representa- tives of the Press," the report went on, " and was suffering from real or assumed deafness. He declined to answer any questions, but advised the interviewers to keep an eye on the New York Daily Boom. The meaning of this advice is un- derstood to be that an enterprising representative of the journal named has obtained an exclusive interview with Mr. Blutterhy." Clarence Hedway looked up from the reading of this paragraph with an amused. smile. " Oh, yes, you will say, perhaps, that this idea about the Daily Boom was port of your fun with the interviewers," burst out Tapton Flutterby, with more than a suspicion of wrath in his voice. " But now read this, and then explain the two things together if you can." " This was an evening paper, in which a New York telegram was marked. In this despatch the vigilant Reuter told bow the Daily Boon, of that morning had published a cablegram • from its special correspondent in London, giving the sub- stance of an interview he had had with 'lepton Flutterby on the author's return to England. The interview related to modern literature, and the novelist had indicated what he bad considered to be his own place among contemporary writers. What he had said on this point was given in summarised form, amd a full report of the inter- view was promised by the next mail. The suc- cessful enterprise of the Daily Boom, added Reu- ter, in thus securing what was, if authentic, the firsl actual interview with the great writer for some five years past, had created qaite a sensa- tion in journalistic circles. Clarence Hedway looked up again, still mil- ' ""

‘" Will you answer me one question, sir ?" thundered Taptou Fiutterby. " With pleasure, if is a reasonable one." " Were you the sender of that message to the Daily Boom ?" " I Wag."

Well -rim —! Then who on earth are you, sir ?"

"Clarence Hedway, of New York. payer. stainer—that is to say, interviewer for the Daily Boom."

_ " Upon may word, sir, you are cool, even for an American pot/mast ! And yen have dared to look me in the face—to accept my invitation to dinner—after abusing my confidenee in this way ! en v. do you eall yourself a genileawn, " will answer you seriatim, Mr. Flu Ely. see, no reason why I should not dare to look you in the face, for I am conscious of no terrible breach of confidence. You told me yourself that it was not the interview you objected to so much as the average interviewer. As to your confi- dences, you will do me the ;justice, perhaps, to recall the fact that it was not I who invited them, but you who bestowed them unasked. Was L not justified in assuming, therefore, that my per- sonality was not distasteful to you, and that it, consequence your objeetion to being interviewed did not apply in this case F" But the subject-matter, sir—the subject- matter ! " Excuse me, Mr. Flutterhy, if I take excep- tion to the imputation that I ' drew ' you on the subject. You volunteered your views, and natur- ally I was glad to listen to them. As to whether I ought to print them in full without your con• sent, I must confess that I have had my qualms. You will notice that all I have done so far is to record in joy cablegram your own estimate of your literary worth—an estimate which . does credit to your modesty, inasmuch as it falls short of the verdict of the whole English-speaking race."

"Then am I to understand," asked Tapton Flutterby, in a somewhat mollified tone, "that you are prepared to withhold the full report of the so-called interview from publication P" "Not quite that," answered the !onrnalist.

" I owe a duty to my editor, who had confidence enough in me to send me at some expense on a mission from which so many distinguished inter- viewers have returned baffled. But I came here this evening prepared to do for you, Mr. Flutter- by, what I would have done for no one else. I have conceived a real respect —you would perhaps object to my use of the word liking '—for you in the course of our few days together on ship- board. The mail for the States leaves to-mor- row. I have brought the ' copy' of the inter- view in my pocket, intending to submit it for your inspection, on one condition. But I find you are in no mood to make terms. For that I am sorry. The copy must be posted to-morrow, and so it will go as it is. I will not inflict my company upon you at dinner ; that would be but a sorry farce. Good evening, Mr. Flutterby." And Clarence Hedway took up his bat to go. " Stop ! Stop! Mr. Hedway," said Tapton Flutterby with renewed agitation. "If you are prepared to let me see the copy and revise it, that alters the situation very materially. Will you name your conditions ?"

" The condition is that if you strike out any of the copy you shall introduce fresh matter equiv- alent in bulk, and shall empower me to say in the introduction that the interview is an authorised one."

Tapton Flutterby pondered a moment or two ; " You are a hard bargainer, Mr. Hedway," he said at last, " but—there, I accept your offer and the condition attached to it. Come in to dinner, man, and we will go through the copy after- wards. Hang me, but I admire your straight- forward business capacity almost as much as your truly Yankee impudence !" It was a cheerful little dinner-party of two, after all. Under the genial influence of the champagne the author took a more generous view of the situation,.and quite renewed the familiar manner he had assumed towards the journalist on the voyage over. "Thank you !" said Clarence Hedway, with real gratitude, when they had spent an hour over his MS. together, and Tapton Flutterby had made sundry emendations, followed by additions to match according to contract. "Thank you. 'The story' is ever so much more readable than it was before, besides having the advantage of being authorised. And now, with your per- mission, I will be going." "Is there nothing else you want?" asked Tap- ton Flutterby jocosely. " Oh, never mind about that ; it can wait." "What on earth do you mean, man? What can wait ?"

"Why, that little matter of the fifty pound bet. You can let the story appear in print first, if you like, and send the cheque on to me at the Daily Boom Office, New York." " Well, I'm—!" For the second time that evening Topton Flutterhy nearly committed him- self. "The bet—l bad forgotten all about it ! Why, of course, I have lost it !" He insisted on making out a cheque there and then, and forced it on Clarence Hedway's accept- ance.

Mr. Bradshaw reported thatat the end of the last half year there was a balance in hand on the poor rate account of £361 16s. 3d. on the special rates account there was a debit balance of £6 15s. 10d. The special expenses for the last half year were £275 and he had received a notice that for the coming half year the call would be increased by £lO3. He was afraid the rates would have to go up about lid. in the Z. On the pre- sent half year there would be a balance in hand of about £lO and he proposed writing to Mr. Ashton asking him if he could not reduce his call to that extent and thereby not necessitate so large an increase in the rates.

Mr. Whitley : I think Mr. Ashton should explain where the money is going. Mr. Hallows: There will be nothing but paupers and officials just now. Mr. Clarke: We want to be watchful about expenses under the special rate. Mr. H. J. Westbrook moved that Mr. Ashton, clerk to the Runcorn Rural District Council, be asked to give the items which constituted the increase in the special rates.

Mr. Bouchier asked whether it would not be better to ask Mr. Ashton for a comparative statement of the last half-year's expenditure and the expenditure for the coming half-year. Mr. Westbrook's motion, with Mr. Bouchier's sugges- tion in corporated was carried. Mr. Bradsha,wl explained that the total calls were £1,450 as compared with £1,179 for the past half year. Mr. Clarke referred to what he termed the disgrace- ful way in which money supposed to be for education was dealt with. A great deal of the money was going not in education but in expenses. One school master told him he had now so much clerical work to do that teaching in hi a schools was entirely out of the question.

THE RUNNING OF WORKMEN'S CARS.

A letter -;;fas--r-eWcl-ir-ori-l-the -"l'O-Wri.-Cfer-k—Oi Warring- ton in which he said that while the Parish Council had no locus stanii under the Light Railway Order, he might say that the question of running cheap cars for the working classes would be considered when the line to Stockton Heath was completed. Mr. Clarke said it was well-know a to everyone, if not to Mr. Whittle, that the Parish Council had a locus standi which was recognized by the Light Railway Com- missioners. They were satisfied if the question of workmen's cars would be considered but they must see that the ratepayers of Stockton Heath, especially the working classes had all the benefits whioh they were supposed to be able to reap under the light railway order. Mr. H. J. Westbrook said that when the line was completed he would certainly move that something be done. When the line was opened for traffic he would move that the Corporation were brought to a sense of their duty with regard to the provision of cheap cars for the labouring classes. Someone would have to make a reouisition for cheap cars to be run and the Board of Trade would inquire into the matter and make an order for the running of cheap cars. It was ridicu- lous to suppose that the people on only two of the tram- way routes in the town desired cheap cars. There were a large number of workingmen in Stockton Heath and he for one would certainly see that the Corporation performed its duty under the order to those men. (Hear, hear). This concluded the business of the meeting.

DIGESTION.

Machnow Praises Bile Beans.

Prize Distribution at Moore. An enjoyable and successful event was the annual prize distribution in connection with the Moore Wesleyan Sunday school which took place in the schoolroom on Friday evening. There was a very largo audience. The programme consisted of hymns by the scholars, and recitations given in excellent style. The prizes, about 75 in number, and consist- ing of handsome books, were distributed by Mrs. Manning. The closing item was a very amusing dialogue "Coaxed and Hoaxed," which was given by the Misses M. Hickman, J. Eboiall, and A. Barrow, and Messrs. James Banner, jun., F. Jones, and James Antrobus. The organist, Mr. John Banner, was pre- vented by indisposition from accompanying the hymns and his place was taken by Miss Susan Banner. The school is reported to be in a flourishing condition, the books recording the names of about 110 children.

IVE INTERVIEWEW,

WALTER E. PINE.

The liner had cleared New York hatheerr, s. was heading out to sea for her five daps' trt. across the Atlantic when Tapton Flutterby, th popular novelist, came on deck. He had beia shut up in his state room for four soljr/ hourei during which time be had successfully " sported the oak " against pushing interviewers innumer- able. Now that they had all most assuredly waged from troublin7, he sought the fresh air and resumed his wonted interest in his kind.

For Tapton Flutterby was not the enemy of man in general, but only of that particular sec- tion of the human species known as interviewers. He had suffered at their hands, as popular heroes will if they happen to be in the slightest degree modest and retiring ; and in his case suffering had bred resentment, and resentment had devel- oped into positive hatred. With the growth of his popularity as an author, the dodging of in- terviewers had come to be a daily cross with him, although one must admit that he took a sort of grim delight in bearing it, since he managed SO warily and consistently to elude and rebuff hie tormentors of the Press. It was his proud boasi that for five years past not a single authentic in- terview with him had been published, for the lery good reason that never an interview had been granted. Tapton Flutterby tow stood on deck at peace with the world, and drank in the vision of a glorious sunset, which was also absorbing the at- tention of many of his fellow-passengers. At last the orb of day sank below the horizon, the bright panorama of the sunset glow faded im- perceptibly away, and the stars came out to keep their nightly vigil. Tapton Flutterby was awe- struck and silent at this familiar yet ever-won- drous display of nature's handiwork. But a young man who stood near him —a man of about his own age and biiild—was moved to put the scene into words. The author overheard him re- cite in low soft tones the lines :

"Come, Evening, once again, season of peace, Return, sweet Evening, and continue long I. Methinks I see thee in the streaky west, With matron step slow moving, while the sight Treads on thy sweeping train." Tapton Flutterby, who was given to poetry himself, scented here a congenial spirit, and WU fain to make his acquaintance. " A poet, air ?" he ventured aloud, with an ingratiating smile. The stranger gave a start, as of one caught unawares. "Oh no, sir," he replied, with a slight flush; "I was merely thinking aloud from Cowper." " A real poet, that," said Tapton Flutterby, " and one too little read nowadays, I am afraid." " Oh, now.ulays," re,ponded the stranger' a' Ii l& contemptuous shrug of the shoulders, " poople make the mistake of reading only books that are fre.h from the press. They waste their days in seeking for new geniuses, and quite neg- lect ;di the grand old classics that have stood the test of lime."

" There is a great deal of justice in what yet wtty," agreed Taptou Flutterby. " Far be it from me, however, to condemn the reading of modern works entirely, since by reason of thee practice I live and move and have my being. That is my card, sir. You may have heard the Dame before."

The author waited expectantly for the stranger's recognition of the magic name, Tapton Finite/4)y, which the peoples of two hemispheres delighted to honour. But he waited in vain. The stranger spelt out the name, as it were, and gave no sign of recognition. " Tapton Flutters by," be said, meditatively, and then added : " May I auk, sir, what is your particular line as en author ?"

This was indeed something fresh. Tapton Flul- terhy felt his amour propre just a little wounded. Ile hardly knew at first whether to resent tilt ignorance of his new friend or to dispel it. After It moment's hesitation, he chose the latter course.

" I am a novelist,'''he iaid, " and a short story writer. Some people honour me—too much, confe—by declaring that my verses are poetry." "No doubt, no doubt,' said the stranger, a little ambiguously. "I am honoured by you* uctilluintnnee, Mr. -- Flutterby. That is sir eArd, sir." _ . .

_ , Tipton Flutterby took it, and read the simp:C se to alinement, " Clarence He way, New York." " I cannot claim to belong, like yourself, to fig world of letters, Mr. Flutterby," continued his new acquaintance. " I just make my living as s pier-stainer; but my work, like yours, go' into many homes, and resembles it, I hope, ie I!' respect, at any rate—by giving satisfaction.'' This little joke served to place the two men %till more en rapport. Presently the talk drifted into purely literary channels. Tipton Flutterby was charmed by the extent and variety of his companion's reading, but was amazed to find that it stopped short of the present generation o/ writers. Even their names appeared unknown to him. _ _

"I figure it out in this way, Mr. Flutterby," explained his fellow-voyager. " Although, I do a good deal of reading, there are so many hun- dreds, or thousands, of acknowledged classics which I have not read yet. Why should I leave the substance for the shadow by neglecting them 31a favour of the works of living authors who may or may not hand their names down to posterity ? Tapton Flutterby admitted that there was something in this argument, but avowed his gratification that it was not universally adopted. To which Clarence Hedway replied, with a laugh, that be supposed it was just as well for present-day writers that everyone did not abstain from reading their works until they had attained to undisputed classic rank. This was not the only such conversation the two men had in the course of the voyage. Tapton Flutterby, like ,the generality of writers, WU fond of talking '44 shop " when he could do SO without fear of his talk being reported in the Press. In Clarence Hedway he found a truly congenial spirit—one who could listen attentive- ly and intelligently, and who limited his OWO share in the conversation to throwing out sug- gestions as pegs on which his companion could bang fresh discourses. " I am afraid I bore you with my views," Tapton Flutterby said on the third day of the voyage. "You know at least as much as Ido aboat these old authors. Now, it is my weak- ness to talk on literary matters ad lib, whenever I cast get anybody to listen. But I should like to say something that is new to you. Suppose wa talk about living authors ?" "As you please," replied Clarence Hedway. "I like to hear you talk anyway, because you throw fresh light on things. But if you turn yourself on to living writers it must be 11. ease of master and pupil between us, and you must not expect me to have any views of my own to offer." Nothing daunted, Tapton Flutterby plunged into the exhaustive subject of modern literature, explaining and quoting as he went. He felt he was doing a service to society in opening pp a new world of thought to a man of Clarence lied- way's intelligence. He spoke fully and critical- ly of modern literature in general, and of his contemporaries in particular. In a burst of confi- dence he even went so far as to indicate his own true status as en author. The rank he assigned himself was sensibly lower than the one he held according to the popular voice. "You have a masterly and convincing way Of OUtting these things, Mr. Flutterby," said his pupil at the end of one of these long and inter- esting monologues. "You lecture on modern literature, I suppose." "Lecture ! No, indeed," Tapton Flutterby replied. "My platform experiences are con- tined to the OCettBioll9 on which I read extracts from my own works." " Bat at least you give the world the benefit of your views in magazine articles ?" " But be is often a clever man," said Clarence Hedway ; " clever, I mean, at getting what he wants. You will find yourself interviewed one of these days, depend upon it." A DANGER TO TILE PUBLIC. At the Warrington Police Court yesterday William Swoddle, a stranger, was charged with being drunk whilst in possession of a loaded revolver. The evidence showed that prisoner was arrested for being drunk and on the way to the police station threatened the officer who took him into custody. When searched it was found he had in his possession a loaded revolver of a large size which was in perfect working order. Prisoner was sent to gaol for fourteen days with hard labour.

SCIENCE NOTES AND

GLEANINGS.

According to M. Sykore, the speetrana of the aurora borealis does not resemble any known spectrum, but there is a certain rela- tion between auroras and solar activity. The phenomenon recalls the illumination of a rarefied gas in a tube under the influence of electromagnetic induction.

A: clock suitable for a sick-room has been devised by Professor Hirth, of Munich. Pressing a button lights an electric lamp be- hind the dial, and throws the shadow of the hours and hands, magnified, upon the ceil- ing, so that invalids can see it from bed Without craning their necks.

MEASURING THE WAVES. The size of the Atlantic waves has been carefully measured for the Washington Hydrographic Bureau. In height the waves usually average about 30ft., but in rough weather they attain from 40ft. to 48ft. In storms they are often from 500 ft. to 600 ft. ion, and last ten or eleven seconds, while the longest yet known measured half a mile and did not spend itself for twenty-three seconds.

ORIGIN - ORIGIN OF RADIUM.

Sir William Ramsay, in a lecture on radium, referred to the fact that not only is the proportion of radium to uranium in rocks wherever found apparently the same, thus pointing to the uranium origin of radium, but also that the amount of helium in the composition of rocks possibly affords an internal record of the rock. It is too early to offer a reliable assertion as to the origin of radium, yet there is reason for believing that uranium is its parent and predecessor. Possibly, therefore, uranium is an unstable element, and slowly degenerates atom by atom into radium, the time of half loss be- ing, say, a billion years. Radium again degenerates into helium and other products, Which in turn go through relatively shorter Periods of degeneration into more stable but relatively de-energised forms of matter. It was pointed out that the evidence was strong in favour of the belief that the energy liberated by radium and its emana- tions was internal energy of degradation and not energy reflected from without.

===== 0002601_19050401_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
SOIL BACTERIA.

According to the well-known fable, a tiny Mouse was the means of saving the life of a lion, when the king of beasts was caught in the meshes of a net, the moral, which old rEsop intended to teach, being thlt small things are sometimes very useful, and should not be despised. How true this is o: the microbes in the soil. Fertile soils are crowded with these minute organisms, in- visible to the naked eye, and yet so actively beneficent that without their incessant in- dustry the soil would become barren, and life, both vegetable and animal, would not exist. There are various specific kinds; some attack vegetable refuse, and some pro- duce the disintegration of animal remains, and there are other groups always present in fertile soils. There are the nitrifying microbes, whose special object seems to be to transform the ammonia produced in the first stage of decomposition of organic matter into nitrate compounds ready for assimilation by the plants; then there are those which crowd into the nodules of leguminous plants, utilis- ing the nitrogen of the atmosphere, furnish- ing the crop with this stimulzing food, and enriching the soil; and there are also others of a destructive character to whose activity is due the loss of nitrogen in manure heaps. The study of bacteria is of comparatively re- cent date; but scientists are now well on their •uar iknow lectge of them.

===== 0002601_19050401_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
LIGHTHOUSE ILLUMINATION.

Heligoland Lighthouse carries one of the greatest searchlights actually in use, and its candle power is placed at the rather incom- prehensible figure of 30,000,000. Such a figure conveys very little, but the Schuckert Com- pany of Nuremburg, which built the light, have, says Knowledge and Scientific News, con- structed a still larger one, for which they claim an illuminating capacity equal to 316,000,000 candles. If it were possible to set this giant on a tower 300 ft. high its rays Could easily be detected eighty miles away; and those who cherish fancies about light telephony can even imagine that conversa- tions could be made audible by it at such distances. The searchlight has a diameter of tft. 6in., which would be a large size for a church clock, and it is built with an iris shutter, such as some modern cameras have. The leaves of the shutter slide with a fixed diaphragm in the axis of the ray of light, and the electrical control is such as to en- able the shutter to govern the movement of the beam of light in horizontal or vertical directions. By the side of these the flash light installed at St. Catharine's Point seems but an insignificant beacon, for it is only of 15,000,000 candle power. But it is five times os powerful as the light it. replaces. The lens has been made in Birmingham. The revolving portion of -the mechanism, instead of being mounted on rollers as hitherto, floats in a big trough of mercury, and rota- tion is easily and accurately brought about bY a clockwork mechanism of a kind not un- like that in old eight-day clocks. The electri- cal energy of the light is derived from the lame magneto-electric generators which have been working for seventeen years now with- out a breakdown.

THE FREE CHLTRCHES.

AFTER THE NATIONAL COUNCIL.

The meetings of the National Free Church Council at Manchester were characterised by a vigour and sustained enthusiasm such as is seldom reached oven by great national assemblies; on all hands it is agreed that in spite of the length and number of the meetings the dull passages were hard to find. The at- tendance of the delegates was throughout admirable, the speeches and sermons were of a high order, and altogether the Tenth Annual Council was eminently successful. Now that people have had time to analyse their impres- awns, it is only natural to ask what permanent asset these meetings will contribute to the Free Church world. Apart from Education and Temperance, which necessarily occupied a good deal of the attention of the delegates, it is felt that two or three objects brought into promi- nence at the Sessions will be greatly benefited, and will receive needed stimulus during the year.

===== 0002601_19050401_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
FARB CHURCHES AND THE MISSION FIELD.

One result of the meetings should be a great awakening with regard to mission work. Dr. Horton's presidential pronouncement devoted considerable attention to the missionary problem. A paper given by Dr. Wenyon also, stimulated thought in the same direction. Dr. Horton believes that a church without the missionary spirit can never find its truest life, and he makes this principle apply to a Federation of Churches. Accordingly, he will do all he can during his year of office to foster interest in the foreign field. The suggestion made by Dr. Horton that the Free Church Council should appoint a Missionary Coinuti ttee wig probably be carried out at an early date. It is expected that a Secretary will be appointed for this specific purpose, whose duty it will be to arouse the ititerest of the Councils in missionary enterprise. Of course it is under- stood that the National Council will not directly engage in Missionary operations, although this course has been urged upon it from son', quarters. The aim will rather be to leno: assistance to the sooieties already tit work. Those acquainted with the circumstances of these organisations, and the need for the work, will agree that much might be done in this direction.

DO ANIMALS THINK? Writing on this subject in Harper's Vo'nehly, Mr. John Burroughs remarks that We ao habitually impute thought to animals that we come unconsciously to look upon them as possessing this power. Thus the dog seems to think about his dinner when Prompted by hunger, or about his home 'and kie master when separated from them. The bird seems to think about its mate, its nest, Its young, its enemies. The fox seems to think about the hound that it hears baying "nPon its track and tries to elude it; the beaver ' seems to think about its (lane the muskrat about its house in the fall, the woodpecker about the cell tti the dozy limb which it will need as a lodging-place in the winter. That is, all these creatures act as if they thought. We kno v that under similar conditions we think, and therefore we impute thought to them. But of mental images, concepts, processes like our own, they probably have none. In. rte or inherited impulse, which we call '",sullet, and outward stimuli, explain most of the actions of the animals. When an animal does something necessary to its self- Preservation or to the continuance of its aPecies, it probably does not think about it as, a Person would do, any more than the Pant or tree thinks about the light when it bends towards it, or about the moisture when it senclit down its tap-root. Touch the tail of a porcupine ever so lightly, and it springs up like a trap, and your hand is stuck with quills. I do not suppose there is any more thinking about the act or any e, onscious exercise of will-power than there is In a trap. An outward stimulus is ap- plied, and the reaction is quick. Does not elan wink, and dodge, and sneeze, and Leugh, and cry, and do many other things Without thought or will? Ido not suppose 1,1 l e birds think about migrating, as man !Meg when he migrates; they simply obcy an Inborn impulse to move south or north, as POE THE YOUNG PEOPLE. the case may be. He thought no one would deny that they might reasonably I say that if the State was to compel its inhabitants to provide themselves better houses, better education, better methods of sanitation, &c., the State should also I 'devise a means of enabling the people to earn sufficient to pay for that higher standard of living. There were, he contended, two phases of the education question which it would be impossible to deal with thoroughly except by making regular earnings possible. The first was the feeding of under-fed school children nd the second the educational deterioration or even absolute loss of educational advantages which must be the lot of the masses of the worker within a few years of their leaving school. Alluding to the housing problem, Morton pointed out that even if better houses were built, so long as the working classes wore unable to earn sufficient to pay the usual rents the difficulty would exist,and if cheaper houses could not be obtained two or more families must crowd into one house which must in that way soon be reduced to resemble very closely slum property. In the same way the remedy for physical deterioration must surely be a regular wage sufficient to secure the necessaries of life. Military training might be a good thing for the improvement of physique as far as it went, accompanied as it would be by adequate provision of food, clothing, and housing during the period of training, but if the latter condi- tions could be made permanent apart from military training, there could be no necessity for such training for that purpose. Whatever benefit a short period of military training might be, it would to a large extent be neutralized by the return of those so benefited to the usual irregular conditions of employment. What, he thought, they must look to for the dissipation of intemperance was a complete uplifting of the standard of life of the lower ranks of the masses which was dependent upon an adequate means of sustenance. If the State could ensure that there could always be bad by those who wanted it regular employment at a living wage, it could condemn and pull down slum property and if necessary The player cannon only, and leave the red playeir looking two or three strokes ahead build houses to replace those demolished. NEW BOOKS, MAGAZINES, &c.

"The Bell in the Fog and other Stories," by Gertrude Atherton, which has lately been published by Messrs. Macmillan and co., is a collection of ten tales which display to a remarkable degree the versatility of this writer. The three first stories in the book have a more or [less common interest in that they deal with theo- sophical and supernatural subjects, with the scenes laid in Hertfordshire, Yorkshire, and Brittany. "The Greatest Good of the Greatest Number," which follows, tells bow a New York physician, out of regard for the husband, sacrifices the life of a wife whose reason has beep deranged by morphine. "The Monarch of a Small Survey" and Talbot of Ursula" are Californian stories, the former giving the history of two women whose lives were ruined by the selfishness of a miserly millionaire; and the latter recording the unrequited love of an English settler for a beautiful Cal; t.Jrnian. "The Tragedy of a Snob" relates the atteml ts made by a New York bank clerk to enter the select cuclo of" cottagers "at Newport, and the painful awakening that followed. Crowned with One Crest" has for heroine an imaginative American woman, and, for theme, her second marriage with an Englishman. A perfect marriage forms the subject matter of" Death and the Woman "and "A Prologue" describes in dramatic form the fateful meeting between the father and mother of Alexander Hamilton, the American statesman, during the historic hurricane that swept over the West Indies at that period. Miss Atherton's stories are decidedly novel and full of dramatic power, but the anti-climax which is frequently introduced jars upon the reader by its harsh suddenness. The author's intention is evidently to administer a shock, and in this respect she is admirably successful. Those people whose literary taste demands the spice of the unconventional and tragic will find much to please them in Miss Atherton's clever stories. Mr. Frederic Harsison's life of " Chatham," which forms the concluding volume of Messrs. Macmillan's well-known series of Twelve English Statesmen," was published early this mouth. With William the Con- queror, Edward the First, and Cromwell, Mr. Harrison placed Chatham as one of the four great creative states- men produced by our country in eight centuries ; and shows how, by the creation of the Colonial system, Chatham became the founder of the British Empire, and how, for a century and a half, the development of our country has grown upon the imperial lines of Chatham's ideals. Within the compass of a small volume Mr. Harrison provides a vivid account of the statesman whose career must always possess a supreme intesest for Englishmen, for by his fiery eloquence, his great personal ascendency, and his passionate love of all be deemed lofty and true, he raised Great Britain from despondency "to a height of prosperity and glory unknown to any formerfage." Readers of Carlyle's ' Frederick the Great " will remember the eccentricities of that monarch's father. and the curious characters who formed the court circle at Potsdam and King's Wusterhausen. In" The Red Cravat," a historical novel which Messrs. Macmillan have issued the author' Mr. Alfred Tresidcler Sheppard, introduces some of these half-forgotten figures of eighteenth century Prussia. Frederick William I. appears painted a little less favourably than in Carlyle's picture ; Jakob Paul Gundling, the bibulous, much badgered Chamberlain, and David Fassmann are por- trayed ; and the reader makes acquaintance with the giant grenadiers of Potsdam, recruited, by force, bribe, ' or wile, from all the nations of Europe. Part of the action passes at Leipzig during Fair-time. The hero 1 and heroine are English, and there is a strong love interest. The plot hinges on an ingenious scheme of Passmann's which, by happy accident, brings about a result the scheme was intended to frustrate. Once one has waded through the decidedly boring account of the amorous Chamberlain's milk and water adventures, it is possible to become warmly interested inthe story. Both hero and;heroine are made of " the right stuff "and one follows the account of their varying fortunes zealously. There is nothing like a big hero,and here we have one large enough of trame to be forcibly enlisted in the Potsdam grenadiersafter a grim struggle for freedom with the wily recruting sergeant and his helpers. We will not reveal the secret of how he ultimately wins his way to the side of love and beauty, but it is a fitting sequel and is well worked out. - - -

One of the most remarkable cases of attempted fraud upon a railway company, in connection with "bogus" personal injuries, resulted only last year in the claimant being prosecuted for perjury and sentenced to a term of hard labour. Out of hundreds of people travelling by a certain train from Euston on a Saturday afternoon, there was only one—a middle-aged man—who com- plained of injury in connection with a slight bump occasioned by shunting operations. Had he been con- tent to claim moderate compensat ion, he would probably have been successful, for the "bump" undoubtelly occurred, and his injuries were very clevsrly simulated. But he estimated his damages at the high figure of

£5,000, supporting his claim by statements as to the large income he had been making in various businesses, to which he was na longer capable of attending. Inquiries into his commercial career had consequently to be instituted by the solicitor's department of the London and North-Western, with the result that an extraordinary story of bankruptcy, fraud, and deceit was unfolded. When the trial came on at the High Court, the plaintiff's evidence-in-chief I was absolutely torn to shreds in cross-examination, and the jury dismissed his suit without hearing the witnesses for the railway company. A warrant for perjury was promptly obtained, and a few days later the man was arrested just as he was leaving his home at Brighton to embark for America. He was tried at the Old Bailey, found guilty by the jury without their leaving the box, and sentenced by Mr. Justice Bigham to nine months' hard labour—certainly not a severe sentence, considering that his action had cost the London and North-Western Railway Company over .£l,OOO, and had engrossed almost the whole attention of their assistant Common Law solicitor and his staff for many month s.—From!an interesting article on "Railways and the Law," which appears in the April "Windsor Magazine," a sumptuous number containing a large instalment of Mr. Rider !laggard's wonderful Ayesha : the sequel to 'She," and a complete story in Agnes and Egerton Castle's fascinating new series "If Youth but Knew," besides many short stories by favourite novelists. The new "Harmsworth Encyclopedia "is an added testimony to the enterprise of the Harmsworth firm. Most people would like to possess a lgood encyclopedia but only the few can afford what is undoubtedly a luxury and necessity in one. Now, even the humble workingman can arange to hare such a magnificent work on his bookshelf as the " Harmsworth Encyclopedia," seeing that it is being issued in fortnightly parts at a cost of 7d. per number. This works out at only one half-penny a day, the total cost of the edition being a little over Li. We have been favoured with advance copies of the first three parts and we are very pleased with their general style and get-up. Paper and printing are excellent, while the profusion of good illustrations is such as to make the encyclopecha one of the most agreeable works of reference available. Each of the 40 parts will contain 160 pages, and the whole will make eight handsome volumes with 6,400 pages and 50,000 entries. As the publishers, Messrs. T. Nelson and Sons and the Amalgamated Press, Limited, say, the encyclopedia "gives the pith and essence of the world's accumulated knowledge." We advise our' readers to cake up the encyclopedia at once.

A PROPOSED SEVERANCE. ,

FAIRPLAY.

The Bases of Free Trade. Sir,—ln my last two letters, I have endeavoured to treat the Fiscal question purely as an abstract one, by exposing a very commen fallacy as to the function of money, or the part that it plays in commerce, and in showing that it is not necessarily synonymous with, but a symbol of wealth, and that it is in fact a measure of value and a medium of exchange within the country in which it circulates only. Also, that in international exchange, excess in the 'slue of imports over the value of exports is not, and in fact, cannot be, paid for in money, but, in the case of a creditor country which ours very largely is, is paid for in interest on investments, services rendered, such as carriage, and exports, which are of course entitled to profit, and that so far from being impoverished, we as a nation are en- riched by this excess. Further, that taxes imposed on imported goods are not paid for by the sender, but by the consumer and therefore are a burden to him. That we did not adopt Free Trade as a policy from any chimerical motives of philanthropy to the foreigners, but simply for the benefit of our own people, in short, from motives of self interest, and that we partisans to it, advocate its continuance for the same reason. I now propose to produce concrete evidence in support of my contention. This meets me in such abundance, _ that 'I find it difficult to make a selection. The agregate I THE PRESIDENTIAL ADDRESS. value of our total imports for the two years 1902 1903 was £1,070,000,000 or an average of £535,000,000 Mr. JXFFERY said he thought it was scarcely neces- per year,whilst the value of exports for the same period sary for him to say how deeply sensible he was of the , high honour conferred neon him to occupy the presi- was .£709,500,000,0r an aversr of ab0ut£354,730,000 per I decide' chair. Mr. STANSFIELD moved "That this association Severs its connection with the Lancashire County Association of Teachers." The formation of the Ashton-in-Maker- Held Association had, he thought, done away with the need for a continued connection with County associa- tion. It was for the purpose of helping the 'Lancashire county teachers that they joined the County Association. Some discussion ensued, when it was decided to adjourn the question to the June meeting of the Asso- ciation.

There was not, he took it, a certain position in the scheme of life assigned to each child and which that child alone must occupy. Instances might be given iii plenty where circumstances and social conditions had not limited the aspirations of children of ability. Education was a natienal concern and the mental capacities of each child were a national asset of the greatest value. Regarded not in its purest and noblest sense,but from a utilitarian standpoint only education proliteth the nation. It was a siagular fact that while a scholar cost £3 per annum, a pauper cost £l5, and a criminal cost £3O. Therefore it behoved those anxious for the national well-being to see that no obstacle was placed in the wry of a child's acquiring the best education possible. The report went on to speak somewhat slightingly of the variety of subjects taught in the elementary schools. Surely this, showing as it did the necessity for the teacher being highly qualified in many subjects. called for a truer appreciation of the value of his ser- vices and a better remuneration for them. Might not the scarcity of teachers be attributed to the fact that their professional training was long and laborious, and hitherto their services had been appraised at no higher value than clerks ? Sir William Anson, admittedly an authority on such a subject, had said that the great difficulty of the Board of Education was the wastage of teachers whom it was impossible to keep in the pro- fession. Why was this wastage and where was the remedy to be sought ? Not in offering inducements in the shape of scholarships and bursaries to the rising youth to enter the profession, but rather by rewarding those in the profession in a manner commensurate with the value of their service. (Hear, hear.) Then, it seemed to him, would the profession prove attrac- tive to the clever youth. The demand for efficient teachers and the limited supply had not placed a ficti- tious value on their services. It might, perhaps, be in- structive to point out that since 1876 the number of boy pupil teachers had decreased 57 per cent., proving at once that the teaching profession did not offer such attractions as some would have th-m believe. As for the statement that the results obtained with this in- creased expenditure were no better than formerly the writer of the report would have done well to give his authority for that. The true value of education was not to be assessed in pounds sterling, nor were its fesults to be looked for in the short space of two years but rather in two decades. Ever remembering that the children of to-day were the men and women of the future, let them endeavour to provide opportunity for every child to realize to the full those abilities with which it had been endowed. (Applause.) Mr. WALLINGTON Said it WAS their duty to thank the President for his admirable address. (Hear, hear.) He agreed with all he had said, and one thing particularly he would like to refer to—the new school to be built in the town. He occupied the position of master of what might be looked upon as a barrack school, and speaking from five or six years' experience of it he could say it was impossible for a man to come into that individual reLationship between himself, the teachers, and scholars, which was desired. He also hoped that when completed the committee would encourage the class teachers of Warrington by appointing to the schools head teachers from their ranks instead of going outside. He was sure they had many fully competent to take control. (Hear, hear.) For the vacancy at Fairfield School an outsider had been chosen, and if he (Mr. Wallington) was living outside the town the only conclusion he could come to would be that the Education Committee did not think there was one local person capable of taking the position. He did not suppose that was the reason for the committee's decision, but still it bore that con- struction at the first look. He hoped they would find a master or mistress from Warrington to take charge of the new schools. (Hear. hear.) Mr. EDDEN seconded the vote of thanks, which Was heartily accorded.

BRITISH WOMEN'S TEMPERANCE ASSOCIATION. Warrington Young People's Temperance Association.

A meeting was held at the Town Hall on Thursday afternoon, at which it was decided to form a Warring- ton Young People's Society in connection with the War- rington Branch of the British Women's Temperance Association. Already the new society, like the parent association, promises to be a great success, forty-seven members being enrolled on the day of its formation. Another evidence of the interest which Warrington Women are taking in the work of temperance reform, was shown by the unusually large attendance at the meeting on Thursday. Mrs.GAILNETT presided and expressed her pleasure at the largeness of the attendance and especially that the audience was to a great extent composed of young ladies. If all the ladies present would only work en- thusiastically for the cause of temperance she was sure that meeting would result in the doing of a great deal of good. It was necessary we should have a sober people and she was pleased to see that temperance work was a iyancing and that the medical profession were gradually coming round to their way of thinking. (Applause.) Miss KINGSBURY, of London. delivered a spirited ad- dress in which she emphasied the great need which existed for the advocacy of temperance principles. When they thought of the suffering and misery which was the direct outcome of drink they realised that it behoved them as women to do their utmost to bring about a happier and better state of things (Hear; hear.) In the work of temperance reform women were a great power and it was necessary they should realize this and work hard for the cause which they had at heart. She hoped they would all sign the pledge, not because they thought it necessary but because by doing so they placed themselves in a better position to advance the principles which they believed. Miss Kingsbury was heartily thanked for her address on the motion of Miss BESSiE DUTTON. It was unanimously decided to form a young people's temperance society and a number of members were enrolled at the meeting. Refreshments were served during an interval.

Death of Mrs. John Scott.

Many persons will hear with regret of the death of Mrs. John Scott, widow of the late Mr. John Scott, which occurred at her residence " Hill Crest," Lower Walton, early on Sunday morning. Mrs. Scott was a prominent worshipper at Wycliffe Congregational Church, and up to within a few hours of her death was apparently in good health. Shortly after nine o'clock on Saturday evening she was seized with an attack of apoplexy, and despite medical attention she passed away a few hours later. She leaves a son, and a largo circle of friends to mourn her loss. The funeral took place at Hill Cliffe on Wednesday after- noon in the presence of a large gathering of relatives and friends. The Rev. John Yonge conducted the service both at the house and at the graveside, being assisted at the grw,eside by the Rev. J. S. Hughes.

RUNCORN POSTMASTER PROMOTED. Mr. William Rennett, postmaster, at Runcorn, has been promoted to the postmastership of Goole. During his four years at Runcorn Mr. Bennett has introduced several reforms.

A TWOPENNY REDUCTION IN THE RATES.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Mersey-street Mission.

The services at the above Mission on Sunday last were well attended when the preacher was Mr. M. Llewellyn (Warrington). At the evening service the preacher took for his text the words "Greater love bath no man than this, that a mall lay down his life for his friends " St. John 15 ch. 13 verse. Love was an element of human life known to all yet hard to define Human love was beautiful as displayed by parents. Our Saviour proved His love for mankind in the acts of love and sympathy He performed while on earth such as that displayed in the cases of blind Bartiramas, the man possessed with the legion of devils, the raising of Lazarus and the young man at the gates of Nain, but I the greater love was proved by the sacrifice of Himself on thErCross that by His death all mankind might be redeemed. Was it not therefore only reasonable that the love of the Saviour for mankind should be returned iby mans love for Him. The collection was for the benefit of the Infirmary.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. A crowded open meeting of this Society was held on Sunday last. Rev. John Yonge presided and read the lesson. Mr. W. Beecher Smith of the Y.M.C.A., Man- chester, was the speaker, and spoke upon Millet's Master piece "The Angelus" in a most interesting manner. The painter of the picture, was a poor French- man. The picture at first sight seems to be nothing extraordinary. It represented a young man and a young woman working in the field. It was the hour of prayer and they were not able to go to the church but there in the field, they reverently lifted there hearts to God in thanksgivings. Professor Drummond, spoke of the picture as manifesting God in the hour of worship. Mr. Beecher said there were three leading points in the picture : God, work and love. God was always with them controlling their actions and life ! There work was only complete when they were doing God's will. In conclusion he asked his hearers to help those who might be going astray, by extending to them love and sympathy. Crosfleld's Prin. Band was in attendance and rendered three selections, " Stradella " " Memories of Meyerbeer " and the " Hallelujah Chorus," ia a masterly manner. The fine rendering of the solo parts and the grand rendering of the chorus, were beautiful, and a treat that the brethren and their friends will not soon forget. The Collection was £4 Bs. 6d. The Com- mittee decided to give one guinea to the Hospital and one guinea to the Nurses' Institution. Nine new men- berg were heartily weloomed, making 433 now enrolled. for the new year. Parr Hall Services.

There was a large congregation at the above service on Sunday evening last notwithstanding the inclem- ency of the weather. The subject announced was the "Church and the Labouring Classes." Plunging at once into his subject Mr. Dunnico said that by the labouring classes he meant all who by voice or pen or brain contri- buted to the country's wealth, he meant the busy toilers in the great hives of industry, the busy housewives and even the little children. For this class the Church had a mission and a message if she would only fulfil the one and proclaim the other. No one could deny with any degree of truth, and for his own part he admitted it with profound regret, that the majority of the wage-earning classes were outside the Christian Church. Mr. Dunnico contended that such a state of affairs reflected little credit on the Christian Church. The Lord Jesus Christ was a working man. His apostles were of the same class. Their sympathies were with the poor. The common people heard them gladly. The . Gospel won its earliest triumphs among the working classes. Had it lost its power ? Certainly not. But the Church had. Why? I Some people conter!ied that the present age was less religious than previous ones but that was an opinion he did not share. At any rate he did not think that the working men were intrinsically irreligious and out of sympathy with Christ and all that His name stood far. Sometime ago Mr. Robert Blatchford had published a book wherein he violently attacked the Christian re- ligion but very few working men had taken that book I seriously. Whatever influence Mr. Blatchford had was due not to his anti-christian propaganda but to his ability as a teacher of certain political doctrines. That and that alone was an assurance, that the labour- ing classes might be won for Christ if the churches were earnest in their desire to do so. He did not suggest that the blame was all on one side but he thought that churches were chiefly responsible. Reform must come first of all in the Church but when it did come the reformed church would be the Church before which all outside obstacles would vanish like mists before the rising sun. Amongst otherithings which kept people away from church Mr. Minnie() mentioned the following : The ordinary average church-going type of Christian to-day was no credit to the Master he professed to serve. The Cross was a minus quantity in too many of their lives. It was owing to the discrep- ancy between profession ,and practice in the lives of so many that Christianity was derided as a failure in more than one half of the world. Another reason was that the Church was so crude and so narrow and bigoted in its outlook. Thank God this was being gradually widened but sometimes it involved a bitter struggle. The social gospel bad come to stay said Mr. Dunnico and as soon as Christian Churches recognised that Christianity was not so much an insur- ance policy for the next world but a practical progamme for this then would the Church regain her lost power. The religion which ignored injustice or oppression in national or civic life was but a parody upon the religion of Jesus. Mr. Dunnico in conclusion made an earnest appeal to all present not to ignore the religious faculty in all men. Every social question was at hearts religious one. Never before had he believed in the Cross of Christ as the one solution for sin and sorrow as he did then, but they must not forget that men had bodies as well as souls and that to minister to the former was often an effective means of reaching the latter.

The Late Mr. R. W. Murray. Mr. Robert Wallace Murray, 'formerly resident in Warrington, head of the Belfast firm of tobacco and snuff manufacturers, whose estate has been valued at £208,097, left the residuary estate (about £150,000) subject to the life interest of the widow, to religious and charitable institutions. Of the ultimate residue, one-half (probably about £70,000) goes to the London Missionary Society, and the other half to hospitals for consumption: " The Christian World " says: "Mr. Murray was always a missionary enthusiast, and in his later years, when a curious morbid state of mind troubled his friends, his devotion to the London Missionary Society was almost the brightest spot in his life. With all • his wealth he got little pleasure out of his later years, and a mania for effecting trifling economies was a pathetic symptom of his last days."

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

(By " Ovalist."i

The town is agog with Cup-tie excitement this week, the fortunes of war having drawn Wigan to the , Warrington enclosure in the third round of the : Northern Union Cup Competition. Various causes contribute to the extraordinary interest shown in the encounter. The same teams met at Warrington last season and victory rested with the hotnesters after one of the hardest struggles they have had. Wigan I seemed certain to draw the match, when Dickenson scored the only point in the game five minutes from "time." This year, in League matches, each team won on its own ground. Wigan's cup-tie career has been a remarkable one so far. They were thrown out of the first round by Leigh, and then got through by a re-play at Wigan owing to that most distasteful of things,—a protest on a technicality. Protest followed protest, but Wigan successfully engaged Halifax, and though guilty of a n offence equal to Leigh's, they will escape with a monetary penalty. Wigan may therefore be con- sidered a lucky team. Most people consider tly,y have no right in the Competition and Warrington will endeavour to bring about the balance of justice to-day.

There is sire to be a tremendous struggle, and granted a fine day all records for attendance on the ground should be easily broken. Wigan's visit last season realised £3ls—the record—and the figures should go :very near to .-ElOO to-day. Warrington people have little doubt as to the result. The home team should win, and by a more substantial margin than last season. Weakness forward has proved Warrington's undoing in the cup competi- tion previously, but this season the forwards are powerful enough for anything. If they pack and push ! properly to-day the home backs shoidd have plenty of ! opportunities to find the weak places in Wigan's de- fence. A big lot depends on the front rank men, for there is probably not much difference in the two sets of backs, though the Warrington men usually play a ! more combined game. It is a reassuring fact that the , two centres,lsherwood and Dickinson have lately been showing much of their old-time skill and dash, while D. Davies has profited immensely by the rest which the Committee gave him in February.

llockenhall is expected to be wearing the jersey next week, when he will probably take a trial spin with the Warrington "A " team at Salford. There are more unlikely things than seeing "Tommy" in the semi-final !

One change has been made in the Warrington fifteen, Belton coming in for Cook, who is having a rest. Fish resumes on the left wing, and a lot is looked for from "the irrespressible" who has not distinguished himself of late. Kenyon's good per- formances in recent matches have earned him the right wing position in preference to Berry. The full team should be: Hallam, full back ; Fish, Isher- wood, Dickinson and Kenyon, three-quarters; Davies and Brooks, half backs; Boardman, Preston, Thomas, Shugars, Belton, Swift, Jolley, and Naylor, forwards.

Wigan have only made one change from the side which defeated Halifax, Brown displacing Topping in the forwards. The selected fifteen is: Mason, full back; Leytham, Carey, Harris, and Treharne, three- quarter backs ; Thomas and Trezise, half backs; Barton, Whittaker, Osborne, Brown, Collier, Watkins, Tyrer, and Vickers, forwards.

The referee will be Mr. W. Wenteheon, who has not been seen at Warrington this season.

The league match with Hunslet last Saturday pro- duced an exciting game, the visitors doing their best to atone for the one point defeat at Hunslet, but succnnabing by an exactly similar margin. Warring- ton were all over them in the first ten minutes and were unfortunate in having only one try awarded after crossing the line throb times. Davies scored by a clever individual eff.irt and Preston goalecl. Hunslet played a strong forward game and soon equalised, A. Gold- thorpe improving on a try scored right in the corner by a wonderfully clever kick. Thus at half-time the teams were equal. The second moiety was a ding-dong struggle and a draw seemed ensured until eight minutes from the close. Then Warrington made a groat effort, and brilliant work by Davies, Isherwood, and Kenyon ended in a try to the latter. The wing man went down the left wing in a fashion that only Fish could have equalled and he was heartily cheered for the effort. Hunslet were not a spent force. however, and W. Goldthorpe raised the score to within a point of Warrington's total by a grand dropped goal from near half way. A better shot has not been seen on the Warrington ground for many a long day. Hallam played a great game once more, and saved the forwards, who had the worst of tho argument, a lot of work.

On Tuesday Warrington played at Batley in a post- poned league game. The team was not at full strength, and with Batley in deadly earnest, the result was not unexpected. Batley won by 7 points to nil, Hallam having a kick charged down in the first minute, which produced five points for the home team. Warrington did their share of attacking, hut the shocking weather and the muddy ground resulted in several chances going astray. It was a poor game, and Batley deserved their win.

The result has had an important bearing on the league table, for the two points may save Batley from descent to the second division. Runcorn are certain to go down, while St. Helens, Swinton, aid Wakefield are all in danger of accompanying them. Warrington have lost a place. Leeds going up by virtue of a win at Runcorn on Wednesday. • The League table up-to-date is as follows : —For— —Against— P. W. L. D. G. T. Pts. G. T. Pts Pts. Cycling.

It is still rather early to expect great doings in the cycling world, for despite the bright sunshine of the past few days there has been more than a suspicion of March chill in the atmosphere which has deterred many wheelers from venturing into the country. Those dauntless enthusiasts, the Warrington Cycling Club, opened their season on Saturday, and although the unpleasant change in the weather had a discomfiting effect upon some, the event was, on the whole, i a success. The conditions n the early part of the afternoon were agreeable for riding, and over twenty members made the journey to quaint old Warburton, in addition to a contingent which travelled per motor cars. A capital tea was pro- vided at the Saracer's Head by Mrs. Barff and after- wards an impromptu concert was held, two or three hours being whiled away in 'jovial style. A downpour of rain

signalized the commencement of the return journey and although ninny members braved the elements, others believing that" discretion is the better part of valour" left their machines at Warburton and reached Warring- ton by rail.

The Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Cycling Club will hold their opening run of the season on April Bth when Knutsford will be visited. A splendid list of fixtures has been arranged and there should be some very pleasant times in store for the members. The officials for the season are :—President,Mr. James Dodds; vice- presidents, Messrs. S. Blythe, C. Bromley, and J. Wads- worth; captain, Mr.T. Barlow; sub-captains,Mr.Joseptt Harding and Mr. E. Graham ; hon. treasurer, Mr. B. Pardo° ; hon. secratary, Mr. E. Graham ; committee, Messrs. A. Blythe, James Harding, W. Webb, A. Highley and P. Jackson.

Mr. E. Pickavance, tho hon. secretary of the Warring- ton Cycling Club, attended the annual meeting of the N.C.W. held in London on Saturday. The British Empire Championships were fixed for July Ist., at Glasgow, and it Was decided to bring a Lights on Vehicles Bill before the notice of Parliament. The question of the Leicester cyclist, Read, was brought up and it was resolved not to proceed further with this The most important item on the agenda was the new financial scheme. After a long discussion, the Council being almost unanimously in favour of the scheme, a resolution to reconstruct the finapcial side of the Union was adopted. The treasurer's statement showed a profit of £179 6s. 4d. The most important change in the rules proposed was one compelling first year men to take out licences. A resolution to this effect was moved by Mr. Pickavance and seconded by Mr. Phillips, of Southport but it was lost, the number of votes in favour being just one short of the requisite two-thirds majority.

Commenting upon the Warrington C.C.'s programme for the season, a writer in "Cycling " says :—" A feature of this club is the bowling handicaps and inter- club bowling matches played throughout the season. Our North Country friends are particularly addicted to this form of recreation." Every year trundling becomes more popular with the Warrington cyclists who are convinced that there are fE w more agreeable ways of tiling up the interval between the arrival at some country resort and the return to town. Now that the green at Sankey is set apart for their exclusive use on certain evenings, the probabilities are that they will become still more "addicted "to the healthful sport.

LIGHTING UP TIME.

To-night Sunday Monday 7.49 Tuesday 7.50 Wednesday Thursday Friday

===== 0002601_19050401_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

Last year twenty-six firms were represented in the Warrington Works Bowling Association, and competi- tion for premier honours was keen, the doings of the te tuts exciting considerable interest. Already there is promise of the coming season being equally successful. Fourteen clubs have signified their intention of joining the association, and there is little doubt that number will be greatly augmented before play commences. The works which have expressed the desire of a continued membership are:—London and North Western (last year's champions), Pearson and Knowles, Orford Tannery, Ryland's, Ilarchin'e Tannery, Cheshire Lines, Whitecross, Naylor's, Crosfield's, Longford Wire Works, Richmond's, and Melbourne's, all being repre- sented by delegates at the annual meeting of the Association which was held at the King and Queen Hotel on Monday evening. In addition,the Corporation employs were represented it being their intention to run a team.

Tke first business was the presentation of the auditors report and Messrs. F. Dooley and J. Simms had to face an unpleasant task in recording financial di'liculties owing to irregularities in the accounts which left the Association with a debit balance of something like £6 (in outstanding accounts) which had to be liquidated. In regard to financial matters, they said, the committee had been very lax, though otherwise they had been very hard working men. Considerable discussion, marked with great restraint, ensued upon the report which was adopted. Mr. J. Willett, who presided, announced that he had taken steps to counter-balance the deficit by means of a public subscription, to which the su nof £2 had already been promised. Whatever the result of the subscription, and whether he was out of pocket or not, he said, the outstanding accounts would be paid by him the next day. Mr. Willett's generous announcement was received with applause and it now rests wish the players and other gentlemen interested to see that he does not suffer as the re.ult of his action

'Mr. W.H. Smalley last year took over the office of hon. secretary to the association at a late period and so well did he discharge the duties that on Monday he was unanimously re-elected to the post. A contest occurred for the position of hon. treasurer, but as the result of a vote Mr. George Holbrook was elected by a large m ajority. The secretary and chairman of the committee were appointed trustees. With regard to the office of president, there was no question as to the re-appoint- ment of Captain George Crosfleld. In past years Captain Crosfleld has evinced a great interest in the encourage- ment of healthy out-door sport, and as he has done all that lay in his power to advance the interests of the Works Association, he well deserves the honour which has again been conferred upon him.

Mr. Samuel Taylor last year acted as Ctpt%in Cros- field's deputy and he was re-elected to the °Moe of senior vice-president. Messrs. A. FL Crosfield, C. S. Smith, Finlayson, J. Craik, Fun., G. L. Brimelow, T. Atherton, \V. Rogers, Bunch, E. Draper, J. Forster, T. Baldwin, J. Allison, Bindloss, J. Newton, ,T.Rushworth, Alderman Evans, and Councillor Roberts and liaise were appointed vice-oresidents, whilst Messrs. J. Willett, T. Davies, G. Ball, J. Monks, J. Makin, Daintith, F. Dean, Peters, Siraister, Howarth and McGowan were elected to jform the committee. The auditors were re-elected.

A discussion ensued upon the rules of the Association. Last year the formation of the Warrington and District Bowling League gave rise to a difficulty. Many pro- ficient players are members both of a works team and of a league team and occasions frequently arose upon which they had to decide whether to play for a league or a works team. To avoid this clashing the committee of both associations re-arranged the dates and times of matches. and so well did they succeed that little or no inconvenience was felt. On Monday evening a delegate advocated the re-construction of the rules so as to prevent the re-arranging of fixtures but his pro- posal did not meet with approval. Between the two associations there is bound to be a certain amount of rivalry, and the taking of any step which would tend to cause a strained feeling is to be deprecated. To force a player to decide to which association he would devote his whole attention would bring about such feel- ing, and wonlvl seriously affect both bodies.

If anything the Committee of the Works Association should be given a greater freedom. Arist year, late on a Friday night,there was a serious break down at the Long- ford Wire Works, which resulted in the members of the works team having to work on Saturday, being there- by prevented from deciding their fixture with the Whitecross team, who claimed a walk over. Under the present rules the committee had no option but to con- cede the claim, whereas they ought to have the power of dealing more fairly with teams placed in the unfor- tunate position of the Longford team.

Mr. A. TI. Crosfleld is not one whit behind his brother in the matter of advocating out-door exercise and it was only fitting that he should be re-elected president of the Liberal Club Bowling Club. Mr. Crosfield has served the club inn similar capacity for some time and the members at their annual meeting were unhesitating in again selecting him for the office. Councillors Tinnion and Wilkinson and Captain Crosfleld were appointed vice-wesidents. The club is fortunate in possessing energetic officials and none strive more for the progress of the club than Mr. E. A. Sanders who retains the secretaryship. Mr. J. E. Warren will this year, as last year, superintend the doings of the players. Mr. H. Daintith, at his own request, was relieved of the duties of sub-captain, and Messrs. W. Hewitt and J. Middleton were placed in competition for that office, the choice falling upon the first named gentleman, Messrs. J. Middleton, H. Daintith, F. Dean, T. O'Brien. J. Houghton, J. Gibson, F. Rathbone, J. Davies, A. H. Sanders, G. H. Shaw, and W. Patrick were elected to constitute the committee.

It was decided not to re-join the Warrington and District Bowling League as it was felt that it was un- fair that a team playing in a league match should mono- polise the green and exclude other bowlers and club members on Saturday afternoons. The programme for the season, which opens on April 15th, is nevertheless ave-y attractive one, matches having been arranged with Winnington Park. Port Sunlight and the Conserva- tive and Saracen's Head Clubs.

Hockey. BY R.G.P.

Last week Stockton Heath received a visit from Alderley Ede, and after a level game in which the home side did the more pressing, and showed better work when nearing goal, the match was drawn, the score standing at four all.

The visitors were a lucky team to go home undefeated as for every shot sent in by them, the home forwards sent in three, and seldom have I seen the home quin- tette more deadly when near goal; but luck was against them, and the ball either glanced off somebody's stick or foot when a goal seemed certain. One case worthy of note was, once when W. Richardson shot with all his might from a distance of about six feet from the goal line, only to send the ball yards over the bar. The visitors third goal was a gift, as the home goakeeper thinking the ball was going wide, allowed a slow shot to hit the upright and enter the net. Stockton Heath

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the' country. The visitation of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on the increase. The origin of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form, of Influenza, and can go about the street just as usual. It can hardly be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful 4 1 influence of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they ever will, regain their former health. As a Preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often proved effectual in warding off attacks of this Malady, and always succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under their influence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating with Sudden Flushes. Severe Head- ache, accompanied by difficulty in breathing, and symptoms of Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the Shoulders, and a stiffness and soreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symptoms, know that they are the forerunners of Influenza, and it behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By common consent Patients every where admit that they feel more depressed and miserable after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. The best way to banish these miserable feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic. We can strongly and confidently recommend this Preparation, as it has treated severe cases of Influenza, Heavy Colds, Extreme Weakness, and Langour, with greater success than any other known remedy. With the advent of spring the interest of Warn, golfers has been re-awakened and every day se- numbers of enthusiasts practising hard. The links a Appleton are in excellent condition the tees havin been re-laid and two new putting greens have be laid. Competition for the captain's prize will cora mence at the end of this month, when Mr, Hen Greenall's prize for players whose handicap is 20 all over will also be played for. Mr. Henry Roberts provided a prize for ladies who, it is to be hoped, will manifest a greater interest than in the past.

The Corporation has for some time past been steps to alleviate the distress caused by the lack. employment. In accordance with a special resolutic passed by the Conncil some months ago the various partments have sought so far as is possible to find tvora. for certain of the unemployed, but in spite of all tht has been done in this direction there still reins'," many who suffer from lack of work. To deal with thel a labour bureau will he opened at the Gymnasium 15e5 week between the hours of 7p.m. and 8.30 p.m. on golre day, Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday, when men out f° employment can register their names, employers, labour being at liberty to inspect the books at same time.

'Coming Events. After having been closed for some time for renova- tion and re-erection in the new St. Mary's (R.C.) Church, the organ will be re-opened to-morrow. Addresses will be given both morning and evening by the Bishop of Shrewsbury. The Rev. H. Dunnico will preach in the Parr Hall to-morrow evening on "John Wesley and Methodism." A public meeting to be held in the Parr Hall on Monday evening will be addressed by Mr. H. Quelch, who will speak on "The muddle of military reform and the way out." Mr. H. Roberts will preside. " PitzattEß." CYCLES at 7 guineas startling value.— J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge st., Warrington.— Advt. Personal. The Rev. Morley Stevenson and Mrs. Stevenson were the recipients of congratulations yesterday on the occasion of their silver wedding. None were more hearty in their good wishes than the students of the Training College who presented the Principal and Mrs. Stevenson with a suitable memento of the happy occasion. Warrington and the Ship Canal. The House of Lords have adjourned the hearing sine die of the appeal of Crosfield and Sons and other traders and manufacturers carrying on business at Warrington, and the Mayor and Corporation of Warrington versus the Manchester Ship Canal. ASH for CUNNINGHAM'S HIGH-CLASS MLNERAL ATERS.—Advt. Progressive Labour League. A debate introduced by Mr. E. Lloyd-Jones took place at the Liberal Club on Tuesday evening, the subject being "an eight hours day and the unem- ployed." Next week Councillor Magma is announced to speak on the education question. Teachers' Social An enjoyable social gathering promoted by the Warrington Certificated Class Teachers' Association was held in the Wyclifb Memorial Buildings, Edge- worth-street, last evening and was largely attended. An excellent musical programme had been arranged by Mr, W. H. Outland and each item was well received. "PREMIER" CYCLES at 12 guineas with change Speed gear &c., unequalled.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. Lecture at Bold-street. An interesting lantern lecture on "North-west of Canada " was delivered in Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom on Thursday evening by the Rev. Joseph Howard. A series of beautiful slides, lent by the Dominion Emigration agent, was exhibited, and described in an entertaining way by the lecturer. The views of Niagara Falls were particularly fine. , Warrington Man's Heroism Rewarded. The heroism displayed by Stockton in the recent unfortunate " gassing " accident in Ellesmere street has received recognition by the Liverpool Shipwreck and Humane Society, who have written to the Chief Constable stating that they have awarded Stockton a Byer medal and a vote of thanks.
